2 The Foundation Model

Purpose

Agent stack focus for this chapter.

Why does a capable model still need a precise contract around every call?

Every capability an autonomous agent adds, from tool dispatch and long-term retrieval to sandboxed execution and self-correction, is assembled from discrete foundation model calls. If the interface contract governing a single invocation is ambiguous, every downstream subsystem inherits that fragility. A model proposal may appear syntactically sound and yet violate schema constraints, arrive past its execution deadline, or misrepresent external environment state. Treating the model as an infallible oracle obscures the primary systems reality: ungrounded token predictors produce candidate proposals across an untrusted boundary, not verified state transitions. Reliability cannot reside in the model weights; it must be enforced by the surrounding runtime through strict token budgets, deterministic schema validation, and independent verification. Within the H·S·A exposure space, the single call marks the baseline origin: a single bounded turn, zero carried state, and zero ambient authority.

Learning Objectives
  • Explain the separate responsibilities of the foundation model, inference service, and agent runtime
  • Construct a token-bounded request containing task messages, advertised tools, and generation limits
  • Calculate how temperature and nucleus truncation reshape the candidate distribution without establishing correctness
  • Diagnose response outcomes using completion status, structural validity, permission checks, and task evidence
  • Distinguish the guarantees of grammar-constrained decoding from learned formatting and correct task values
  • Estimate call latency and repeated-input work under explicit serving and prefix-reuse assumptions
  • Evaluate interface representations using accepted outcomes and resource use under matched task budgets

2.1 One Turn of the Repair Loop

A single call runs one turn, carries no state, and holds zero ambient authority.

Yao, Shunyu, Jeffrey Zhao, Dian Yu, Nan Du, Izhak Shafran, Karthik Narasimhan, and Yuan Cao. 2023. “ReAct: Synergizing Reasoning and Acting in Language Models.” International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).

The repair agent (lighthouse 1.1) needs to inspect the connection-pool implementation before proposing a change. A model invocation that emits read_file("src/pool.py") produces only an unexecuted text string. The generation itself carries no side effects, leaving the surrounding runtime to determine whether the path exists, verify that the operation is permitted, execute the filesystem read, and feed the resulting observation back into subsequent prompts. Interleaving model-generated actions with runtime observations forms the core interaction loop formalized by ReAct (Yao et al. 2023). In an agent architecture, that loop decomposes into discrete, isolated inference steps, where the stability of the entire system depends on how the runtime manages each individual turn.

Interactive chat interfaces rely on human operators to resolve operational ambiguity. A person working in a chat window immediately spots a response truncated mid-sentence, evaluates whether a proposed shell command is safe to run, and tolerates variable generation delays. An autonomous agent removes that human supervisor. The runtime receives the completion purely as structured data and must programmatically resolve questions that human judgment handled implicitly: whether the prompt fit within the model’s context window, whether generation completed normally or hit a hard output token ceiling, whether a syntactically valid tool request targets a resource within permitted workspace bounds, and how much latency and financial budget the invocation consumed. Unstructured reply text cannot answer these operational questions. A runtime that attempts to deduce execution state by scraping raw output strings inevitably fails, whether by applying truncated code patches or executing commands outside its intended environment.

Eliminating these failure modes requires a formal call contract between the agent runtime and the model inference service. This contract governs both directions of the interface: the outbound request (specifying message history, advertised tool definitions, and token limits) and the inbound execution record (specifying payload structure, completion stop reasons, and token consumption). It also dictates the defensive validation pipeline the runtime applies before dispatching any proposed action, alongside accounting for the latency and input re-computation costs incurred by each invocation. Rather than assuming model reliability, the contract treats the inference service as an untrusted, probabilistic generator. By recording verified execution facts at each boundary, the contract keeps every control-flow decision with the runtime rather than with the foundation model.

The chapter follows the repair agent’s single inspection call from request to bill, adding one runtime obligation at each step. Before the call is sent, the runtime must know who owns which decision (section 2.2) and whether the serialized request fits with room left for an answer (section 2.3). How the service then produces a candidate (section 2.4) explains why repeated calls differ and why generation can stop before the candidate is whole. That possibility fixes the order of the checks on the way back. The runtime first reads the call’s normalized status to learn whether a complete candidate arrived at all (section 2.5), then asks which property each check on a completed candidate actually establishes (section 2.6), and finally whether syntax errors can be prevented during generation instead of rejected after it (section 2.7). The last two sections price the call. Output length and re-sent history set its latency and work (section 2.8), and those costs decide how competing output representations should be compared, on accepted repairs under a matched budget (section 2.9).

2.2 The Model Invocation Boundary

When an agent issues an operation such as read_file("src/pool.py"), the request traverses three distinct architectural components: the foundation model, the inference service, and the agent runtime. The foundation model1 computes conditional probability distributions over token sequences given a prompt context. The inference service hosts the model weights, executes forward passes on hardware accelerators, applies generation parameters, and packages the resulting tokens into a response envelope. The agent runtime orchestrates the broader task: it constructs the prompt, tracks resource budgets, validates generated candidates, and decides whether an action should actually execute against the host environment. Even when all three components reside on a single physical host, share an operating system process, or run within the same security domain, their interfaces maintain a crucial boundary: generated content remains strictly isolated from the decision to act on it.

1 Homogenization: The same report that named foundation models warned that, because many applications adapt one shared base model, a defect in that model passes into every system built on it (Bommasani et al. 2021). For autonomous agents the warning is concrete. Every runtime that invokes the same model and serving path inherits the same failure modes at once, so the runtime, rather than the model, must hold the operational checks.

Bommasani, Rishi, Drew A. Hudson, Ehsan Adeli, Russ Altman, Simran Arora, Sydney von Arx, Michael S. Bernstein, et al. 2021. “On the Opportunities and Risks of Foundation Models.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2108.07258.
Definition 2.1: Proposal

A proposal is model output that specifies a candidate action or answer for the runtime to evaluate, carrying zero ambient authority and producing no external side effects until the runtime explicitly validates, authorizes, and executes it.

  1. Significance: Every external effect of an agent depends on an explicit runtime decision. Because the model call possesses zero ambient authority, a rejected proposal costs one inference invocation rather than an unintended modification to the environment. That invocation still consumes resources and counts toward \(\AttemptCost\) in the cost per accepted task (equation 1.2).
  2. Distinction: A proposal must not be confused with an effect (a state change executed by the runtime after authorization) or an observation (the runtime’s factual record of what an operation returned). Emitting read_file("src/pool.py") is merely a proposal; the file contents subsequently retrieved from disk and supplied to the next call constitute an observation.
  3. Common pitfall: Logging a tool request as an executed action, or accepting a model’s textual claim that “the file has been read” as an empirical observation, allows fluent prose to stand in for operational evidence.

2.2.1 Messages and tool definitions

The runtime structures interaction with the model through an ordered sequence of role-tagged messages. A system message establishes application instructions, operational constraints, and behavioral policies. A user message specifies the task objective or developer prompt, while an assistant message records earlier model generations. When the runtime executes an action, it supplies the resulting output as a tool message containing the raw observation. Tagging content with a role structures the prompt context for the model’s attention mechanism, but the tag itself provides no security guarantees: role labels organize the token stream, while the runtime remains responsible for verifying whether the content is authentic and whether it adheres to the task contract.

↩︎ The task contract, which fixes the goal, environment, permitted actions, available observations, and completion criteria, is established in The five-part task specification contract.

Alongside the message history, the invocation payload advertises the tools available to the model and specifies their expected argument schemas. In listing 1, the runtime declares a file-reading tool with a single required string parameter. The JSON-schema definition informs the model of the syntax required for valid proposals, but the schema defines only the interface; the runtime enforces workspace sandboxing and filesystem permissions independently.

The model_config identifier explicitly binds the request to a specific model version, weights checkpoint, and serving profile. Sampling controls govern how the inference service selects output tokens from the model’s predicted probability distribution. Concurrently, resource bounds protect the system from runaway execution: max_output_tokens (\(\OutputLimit\)) caps the decoding budget to prevent unbounded generation, while deadline (\(\CompletionDeadline\)) establishes a strict timeout against queue delays and serving stalls. Specifying these bounds in the request record is effective only when the inference service or the client runtime actively enforces them during generation.

When generation concludes, the inference service returns free-form text, a structured tool proposal, or a service error. A runtime adapter normalizes these provider-specific responses into a uniform envelope, as illustrated in listing 2. In this example, the service reports a completed generation pass that produced a structured tool request: a proposal to inspect src/pool.py, representing a single step toward diagnosis before any repair is verified.

Here, kind indicates how the runtime parser must interpret the payload. The normalized status confirms that the transport and generation protocols completed under the adapter’s contract, while stop_reason captures the stop reason reported by the serving engine. This signal reveals why decoding terminated: whether the model generated a designated tool-call sequence, emitted an end-of-sequence token, or halted because it reached the token ceiling. The usage block tallies resource consumption, tracking \(\SequenceLength\) input tokens processed and \(\OutputLength\) output tokens emitted. Bare \(\SequenceLength\) is sequence length throughout this book, here the prompt a call reads; the State levels \(\Glyph{S}_0\) to \(\Glyph{S}_3\) always carry their subscript. Accurate accounting across invocations prevents context-window overflow and provides the raw telemetry needed to monitor operational costs; when an upstream provider omits token accounting, the runtime must record the usage as missing rather than silently recording zero consumption.

Every path out of the response ends in the runtime, and the stop reason chooses the path (figure 1). A tool call goes to validation and authorization before anything runs, a finished answer goes to verification, and output cut off at its limit goes to quarantine, where it is kept for diagnosis and never executed (section 2.5.2 states the rule).

2.2.2 Proposals and observations

When the runtime receives a tool proposal, it must not execute the action blindly. Instead, it routes the payload through a strict sequence of validation and authorization stages. First, the runtime validates structural conformance, ensuring that the proposed arguments match the declared schema (for example, confirming that path is a string rather than an arbitrary shell command). Next, it checks the arguments against the task’s authorization policy: advertising read_file grants the model permission to propose file reads, but confers no right to access arbitrary paths. The runtime must verify that src/pool.py resolves within the task’s allowed workspace rather than escaping to sensitive host locations through directory traversal. Once authorized, tool execution proceeds entirely outside the model invocation, typically within an isolated sandbox or container with explicit execution timeouts.

After the tool executes, the runtime captures the empirical outcome and prepares the subsequent invocation context. It appends the model’s prior assistant proposal and pairs it with a corresponding tool message containing the resulting observation (here, the bounded file excerpt). Where the API supports correlation identifiers, the observation explicitly references the call identifier of the proposal it answers, preventing ambiguity during parallel or multi-tool executions. Operational errors, such as a missing file or an access permission denial, must be captured faithfully as observations rather than swallowed or masked by synthesized content. Models rely on accurate negative feedback to revise their strategies and recover from errors. Furthermore, whenever the runtime truncates tool output to preserve token headroom, the observation must clearly demarcate the omission so that the model does not mistake an excerpt for the entire file.

The model in the subsequent invocation observes the tool output solely because the runtime reconstructs and supplies the updated context window. Foundation models are stateless token predictors: they retain no internal memory of previous requests or intermediate executions. Session identifiers or server-side prefix caching (reusing attention state the service already computed for a prompt prefix that repeats across turns, priced in section 2.8.3) can reduce latency, but the runtime remains the sole authority governing which messages enter the prompt. This separation also exposes temporal subtleties. A stored file excerpt reflects only the state of the workspace at the moment of inspection; if a subsequent command modifies the file on disk, the in-context observation becomes stale. The prompt records historical evidence rather than an active binding to the filesystem.

This operational reality exemplifies fail-plausible dynamics. A model can emit a syntactically pristine proposal targeting the wrong source module, or generate a compelling analysis grounded in an obsolete observation. Emitted tokens often appear convincing even when the underlying reasoning or environment state is completely disconnected from reality. Robust agent engineering therefore requires treating status, schema structure, security authorization, and empirical evidence as distinct, non-fungible properties of the interaction.

↩︎ The fail-plausible fault, output that looks well formed and credible while the intended property fails, is established in The fail-plausible fault.

The operational responsibilities summarized in table 1 emerge directly from these architectural distinctions. These requirements apply universally, whether the model executes within the local process or behind a remote network API.

The request and response records define the operational boundary governing model invocations. Messages supply context, tool definitions define candidate proposals, and the runtime mediates all transitions to external state. Before an inference engine can admit a request, however, the entire payload must fit within the model’s context window and generation limits. Enforcing those constraints requires measuring the request in tokens rather than relying on coarse approximations such as character counts, messages, or source files.

2.3 Discrete Token Representation

When an automated repair agent appends a connection-pool source file and a compiler diagnostic to its message history, the payload expands rapidly. Naive metrics like file counts or raw character counts cannot determine whether the next request will fit within the model’s capacity. Under the hood, role delimiters, tool schemas, system prompts, file paths, and source code all serialize into a single sequence of discrete units before entering the neural network. The true operational budget is the token count of that fully assembled sequence, measured strictly against the model’s specific tokenizer and wire format.

2.3.1 Tokens in the serialized request

A tokenizer maps text into identifiers from a finite vocabulary. Subword methods represent frequent fragments compactly while breaking unfamiliar words and identifiers into smaller pieces (Sennrich et al. 2016). Byte-pair encoding (BPE)2 learns repeated combinations of smaller units; other tokenizers use different segmentation rules, and some learn them directly from raw text without assuming word boundaries (Kudo and Richardson 2018). A token may represent a word, part of a word, whitespace, punctuation, or a combination of these. Token boundaries therefore need not coincide with the boundaries of a source-code identifier or a JSON field.

2 Byte-Pair Encoding (BPE): The algorithm began as a data-compression scheme that repeatedly replaces the most frequent pair of adjacent bytes with an unused symbol (Gage 1994); Sennrich et al. (2016) repurposed the same merge rule to build open vocabularies for neural translation. Because the merges are learned from the training corpus’s frequencies, the token cost of a string reflects how common it was in that corpus, so an unusual identifier or path can split into more tokens than a common word of the same length, and the runtime must count rather than estimate.

Gage, Philip. 1994. “A New Algorithm for Data Compression.” C Users Journal 12 (2): 23–38.
Sennrich, Rico, Barry Haddow, and Alexandra Birch. 2016. “Neural Machine Translation of Rare Words with Subword Units.” Proceedings of the 54th Annual Meeting of the Association for Computational Linguistics (Volume 1: Long Papers), 1715–25. https://doi.org/10.18653/v1/P16-1162.
Kudo, Taku, and John Richardson. 2018. “SentencePiece: A Simple and Language Independent Subword Tokenizer and Detokenizer for Neural Text Processing.” Proceedings of the 2018 Conference on Empirical Methods in Natural Language Processing: System Demonstrations, 66–71. https://doi.org/10.18653/v1/D18-2012.

Indentation alone alters the resulting sequence. Under one widely used byte-pair vocabulary, cl100k_base, for example, the unindented line def get_user_id(): encodes as five tokens beginning with def (identifier 755). Indenting that exact line by four spaces yields six tokens: three leading spaces (262), followed by def with the fourth space fused to the keyword (711), and finally the remaining four tokens. A compiler lexer records indentation as block structure and emits the same keyword token at both levels; the model instead receives a different opening sequence of integer identifiers, with only the four tokens after the keyword shared (figure 2).

In an inspection request, the string src/pool.py functions as a clean path argument at the tool interface, yet it decomposes into a sequence of subword tokens at the model interface. Altering a file path, introducing indentation, or escaping source code inside a JSON payload shifts token boundaries unpredictably. Because these splits depend entirely on the active vocabulary and serialization scheme, neither JSON formatting nor punctuation imposes a constant token overhead relative to raw prose. Systems runtimes cannot infer prompt length from average character-to-token ratios; they must measure the exact byte sequence scheduled for transmission.

Structural framing introduces further overhead. Model APIs wrap messages in specialized control sequences that delineate roles, conversation turns, and tool definitions according to model-specific wire protocols. Counting only the application’s payload strings, such as user instructions or tool outputs, ignores this structural envelope. If the application handles serialization locally, it must tokenize the fully formatted wire payload using the model’s precise tokenizer. Conversely, when a managed remote service injects proprietary chat templates or handles hidden system wrappers, the application must rely on the provider’s token-counting endpoint and documented admission policies. Because token vocabularies and merging rules differ fundamentally across model families, token counts from one model cannot serve as proxies for another.

This accounting discipline applies equally to output generation. A concise tool invocation consumes fewer output tokens than an elaborate textual explanation that embeds the same invocation, but the exact cost depends on how the tool call is serialized. Compact schemas reduce latency and token spend only if they retain the structural detail the runtime demands; stripping essential parameters, file paths, or error enumerations to shave tokens undermines the robustness of downstream execution.

2.3.2 Output headroom

Let \(\SequenceLength\) denote the length of the fully serialized input prompt, \(\ContextWindow\) the total context window (the maximum sequence capacity spanning input and output tokens within a single invocation), and \(\OutputLimit\) the reserved generation allowance, or output headroom. In an architecture where input tokens and generated tokens share a unified context budget, an admission controller must enforce:

\[\SequenceLength \le \ContextWindow - \OutputLimit \tag{1}\]

This inequality guarantees that an incoming prompt cannot saturate the context buffer and starve the model of capacity to produce an answer. Real-world services often impose independent ceilings on input and output lengths alongside this shared context limit, requiring the runtime to satisfy both individual caps and the combined constraint. Reasoning models complicate the count further. They generate intermediate reasoning tokens before the visible answer, which the caller may never see, and those hidden tokens debit the generation budget just like user-facing text. A concise three-line visible response can easily exhaust a large generation reservation if the model generated hundreds of hidden reasoning tokens beforehand.

↪ How reasoning models spend generated tokens on intermediate work, and how the runtime budgets them, is developed in Reasoning models and generation allowances.

Suppose the repair interface has a combined window of 8,192 tokens, and the runtime reserves 1,024 tokens for generation. Its input ceiling is \(\ContextWindow - \OutputLimit = 8,192 - 1,024 = 7,168\) tokens. If the fully serialized request occupies 6,000 tokens, \(7,168 - 6,000 = 1,168\) tokens of room remain for additional input while preserving the response allowance. The limits are scenario values, not a named model’s specification.

If an incoming tool observation exceeds this remaining headroom, the admission check fails. At this juncture, the runtime faces an explicit engineering trade-off: it can compress prompt history, extract a narrower file slice, or route the call to an execution profile with a larger context window. Artificially shrinking the output allowance \(\OutputLimit\) to force admission is risky, as it leaves the model insufficient room to complete complex tool invocations or structured plans. Similarly, silently truncating tool outputs to squeeze them into the remaining margin can strip away the critical stack trace or diagnostic error that the agent needs to resolve the bug.

Even when a request satisfies admission checks, generation can still hit the output token ceiling mid-stream. In systems terms, the runtime must never confuse a syntactically well-formed prefix with a successfully terminated generation. A truncated JSON object may happen to pass a lenient parser if closed artificially, yet it remains an incomplete execution plan. While a partial payload provides valuable diagnostic telemetry, executing it as an intact tool call can cause downstream corruption. The invocation contract in section 2.5 turns this boundary into an explicit rule that reads the call’s status before its payload.

2.3.3 Multimodal input accounting

When the repair task incorporates visual inputs, such as a screenshot of a failing diagnostic dashboard or a user-interface (UI) render, context accounting shifts from character tokenization to multimodal embedding. The model’s vision front-end maps the visual input into a set of context positions. While vision transformers typically partition images into uniform patches (Dosovitskiy et al. 2021), upstream preprocessing steps like dynamic tiling, resolution downsampling, and spatial pooling mean that the final count of reserved context tokens rarely matches raw patch counts.

Dosovitskiy, Alexey, Lucas Beyer, Alexander Kolesnikov, Dirk Weissenborn, Xiaohua Zhai, Thomas Unterthiner, Mostafa Dehghani, et al. 2021. “An Image Is Worth 16x16 Words: Transformers for Image Recognition at Scale.” International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).

Consider the earlier budget when adding this visual artifact. If the service reports that the image and its framing tokens require an additional 2,048 context positions, the total request demand expands to \(6,000 + 2,048 + 1,024 = 9,072\) tokens. This total exceeds the 8,192-token context window by 880 tokens, triggering an immediate admission failure before dispatch. The orchestrator must intervene before calling the model. While downscaling the image can reduce its token footprint under some vision backends, aggressive compression risks obliterating fine-grained diagnostic clues, such as single-character error codes or minute trace graphs. Multimodal token budgeting is thus an optimization over information preservation, balancing fidelity against strict context bounds.

Checkpoint 2.1: Admitting the next repair request

The repair agent is about to add a source excerpt and a diagnostic image to its next call. Its output allowance must remain available for a complete proposal.

Token accounting provides an indispensable gatekeeping mechanism: it establishes an upfront admission test for the complete request while reserving adequate operational headroom for the response. Context admission, however, addresses only initial capacity. Once the model begins decoding, token-by-token autoregressive generation dictates how quickly that reserved headroom is consumed and whether the interaction terminates cleanly or truncates prematurely.

2.4 Autoregressive Generation

A client request specifies the conversation history and the catalog of available tool schemas, but the generated response remains open-ended and stochastic. In a software repair task, for example, the model might propose read_file on a specific configuration path, select an entirely different source directory, or emit an unstructured natural-language explanation instead. Systems engineers must understand how the engine generates this text to configure appropriate token budgets and sampling parameters. That understanding also clarifies why re-executing an API request against a live model produces a fresh distribution of behaviors, whereas replaying a recorded trace provides exact downstream determinism.

2.4.1 Conditional token selection

Autoregressive language models generate text by decomposing the joint probability of a sequence into a product of conditional next-token distributions, a foundational framing introduced in early neural language modeling (Bengio et al. 2003). Let \(\InputTokenSequence\) denote the input tokens and \(\OutputTokenSequence\) an output sequence. Its conditional probability factors as

\[\Pr(\OutputTokenSequence \mid \InputTokenSequence) = \prod_{t=1}^{\OutputLength} \Pr(\OutputToken_t \mid \InputTokenSequence, \OutputTokenPrefix) \tag{2}\]

At output position \(\TokenIndex\), the model with parameters \(\ModelParameters\) produces one score, or logit, for each token in vocabulary \(\TokenVocabulary\):

\[\LogitVector_t = \NextTokenLogitFunction(\InputTokenSequence, \OutputTokenPrefix) \in \mathbb{R}^{|\TokenVocabulary|}\]

A decoding rule selects a token from these scores, and the chosen token is appended to the prefix for position \(\TokenIndex+1\). Because every subsequent step conditions on all preceding tokens, early decisions cascade through the remainder of the generated sequence. When a model emits a specific file path inside a read_file argument, for example, that string immediately conditions the emission of the closing syntax and subsequent reasoning tokens. The model generates this path purely from learned statistical associations without consulting the operating system to establish whether the file actually exists.

In an inference serving engine, the decode loop processes the prompt, computes next-token logits, selects an output token, appends it to the prefix context, and repeats this cycle until triggering a termination condition. Generation can terminate normally when the model emits an explicit end-of-sequence token or matches a caller-configured stop sequence. Alternatively, an execution engine may halt generation prematurely when reaching an output token limit, or abort unexpectedly due to an infrastructure timeout or network disconnect. Serving interfaces must report these stop reasons explicitly: receiving an incomplete JSON object due to an exhausted token limit requires different recovery handling than receiving a cleanly terminated tool call. A clean stop sequence merely signals that the model finished generating text; it provides no guarantee that the underlying task succeeded.

Production Transformer implementations (Vaswani et al. 2017) eliminate redundant computation across decoding steps by maintaining a key-value (KV) cache:3 memory buffers that store intermediate attention keys and values derived from previously processed tokens. Reusing this state avoids recomputing projections for historical tokens at every step. For a fixed full-attention architecture and storage precision, retaining more tokens requires more cache capacity. This cache represents transient intermediate computational state rather than an authoritative audit log proving which tools executed or what their external effects were. Cache organization and capacity planning are developed in KV Cache Management; the direct operational consequence is that the prompt and generated output both contribute to a call’s memory footprint and resource demand.

3 Key-Value (KV) Cache: In causal attention, key and value vectors at a given position depend solely on that token and its predecessors, so once computed they remain static throughout subsequent sequence expansion. Retaining them in device memory turns each new decode step from full-sequence recomputation into a single new row projection per layer alongside a read of stored rows. Consequently, cache capacity and memory bandwidth, rather than floating-point arithmetic, become the primary operational constraints as context windows expand.

4 Speculative Decoding: A small draft model proposes several candidate tokens, and the target model scores them all concurrently in a single forward pass; a modified rejection-sampling rule accepts each drafted token with a probability that guarantees the accepted sequence matches the exact distribution of the target model sampling independently (Leviathan et al. 2023; Chen et al. 2023). This guarantee is distributional rather than bitwise: it reduces the number of accelerator passes per generated token without altering the underlying candidate space, so it leaves the reproducibility questions of section 2.4.3 unchanged.

Leviathan, Yaniv, Matan Kalman, and Yossi Matias. 2023. “Fast Inference from Transformers via Speculative Decoding.” Proceedings of the 40th International Conference on Machine Learning 202: 19274–86.
Chen, C., S. Borgeaud, G. Irving, J.-B. Lespiau, L. Sifre, and J. Jumper. 2023. “Accelerating Large Language Model Decoding with Speculative Sampling.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2302.01318.

Because each generated token extends a dependent sequence, output length is a major cost variable of a call, and the serving implementation sets its realized latency. Although the conditional dependence in equation 2 fixes the mathematical order of token choices, it does not mandate that hardware evaluate each token in an isolated forward pass. Techniques such as speculative decoding,4 for example, draft candidate token blocks with a small auxiliary model and score them in parallel with the target model, provably preserving the target output distribution (Leviathan et al. 2023; Chen et al. 2023). The per-token decode floor derived in section 2.8 is therefore a property of plain one-token-per-pass decoding, not a law of the factorization.

2.4.2 Sampling controls

For positive temperature5 \(\SamplingTemperature\) (subscripted to keep it distinct from the trajectory \(\Trajectory\)), softmax converts the logits into next-token probabilities:

5 Temperature: The nomenclature comes from statistical mechanics, where the Boltzmann distribution weights a state of energy \(\BoltzmannStateEnergy\) by \(\exp(-\BoltzmannStateEnergy/\BoltzmannConstant \BoltzmannTemperature)\); reading each negative logit as an energy makes equation 3 mathematically identical. In thermodynamic systems, high temperature distributes particles across many states, whereas low temperature collapses the system toward its ground state. In language model decoding, scaling temperature balances candidate exploration against exploitation of the mode without altering which token scores highest.

\[\Pr(\OutputToken_t = \VocabularyToken \mid \InputTokenSequence, \OutputTokenPrefix) = \frac{\exp\left(\OutputLogit_{t, \VocabularyToken} / \SamplingTemperature\right)}{\sum_{\NormalizerTokenIndex \in \TokenVocabulary} \exp\left(\OutputLogit_{t, \NormalizerTokenIndex} / \SamplingTemperature\right)} \tag{3}\]

Unit temperature (\(\SamplingTemperature = 1.0\)) leaves the logits unscaled. Lowering a positive temperature amplifies logit disparities, sharply concentrating probability mass on higher-scoring tokens; raising it compresses logit differences and makes the distribution flatter. With a finite vocabulary and finite logits, the distribution approaches uniformity as temperature grows without bound. Systems engineers should treat temperature strictly as an entropy modulator rather than a confidence metric. Because next-token cross-entropy training minimizes sequence prediction error rather than calibrating scores as estimates of task success, high token likelihood does not verify that a completed response is logically sound or correct.

Greedy decoding instead selects the maximum-scoring token: \(\operatorname{argmax}_{\VocabularyToken \in \TokenVocabulary} \OutputLogit_{t, \VocabularyToken}\), combined with a consistent tie-breaking rule. Given fixed logits and a fixed tie rule, that selection is deterministic. While serving APIs frequently expose greedy decoding through a temperature-zero setting, the runtime executes this via direct argmax selection rather than evaluating equation 3 with division by zero. As positive temperature approaches zero, softmax concentrates all probability mass on the maximum; if several logits share the maximum, that mathematical limit alone does not select among them.

Sampling can also restrict which tokens remain eligible. Top-\(\TopTokenSampleCount\) sampling (Fan et al. 2018) retains the \(\TopTokenSampleCount\) highest-scoring tokens, according to a defined tie rule, and renormalizes their probabilities. Top-\(\NucleusProbabilityMass\) sampling, also called nucleus sampling, sorts tokens by descending probability and retains the shortest leading sequence whose cumulative mass reaches threshold \(\NucleusProbabilityMass\) (Holtzman et al. 2020), subsequently renormalizing that active set. Top-\(\TopTokenSampleCount\) fixes the candidate count regardless of context, retaining implausible tail tokens when the distribution is peaked and cutting viable candidates when the distribution is flat. Top-\(\NucleusProbabilityMass\) resolves this rigidity by allowing the candidate pool size to adapt dynamically to distribution entropy. When temperature scaling and truncation are combined, their evaluation order and the runtime’s numerical conventions form an explicit part of the decoding configuration.

Neither filter identifies valid file paths, truthful statements, or safe operations. A rare token may be essential to a correct identifier or software patch, while a highly probable continuation may propose an incorrect file name. Truncation filters alter the distribution of candidates exposed to downstream execution, and evaluation against external task criteria determines whether that modification improves performance.

Nucleus truncation keeps the two tokens whose mass reaches 0.90 and zeroes the gray tail.

Napkin Math 2.1: Temperature and truncation on the same logits
Problem: A hypothetical vocabulary \(\TokenVocabulary = \{\VocabularyToken_1, \VocabularyToken_2, \VocabularyToken_3, \VocabularyToken_4, \VocabularyToken_5\}\) has logits \(\LogitVector_t = [12, 10.5, 9, 6, 3]\), held fixed so that differences arise strictly from the decoding configuration. How much probability mass concentrates onto the top token when halving the temperature, and which tokens survive nucleus truncation?

Math: At temperature \(\SamplingTemperature =\) 1, the exponentiated logits sum to 207596.9. Normalizing each exponential by this partition sum yields the baseline distribution:

\(\UnitTemperatureProbabilityVector \approx [0.7840, 0.1749, 0.0390, 0.0019, 0.0001]\).

Halving the temperature to \(\SamplingTemperature =\) 0.5 doubles the exponent of each term. Following normalization, the probability of the mode \(\VocabularyToken_1\) surges to 0.9503, capturing 95.0 percent of the total distribution. All lower-ranked tokens retain positive probability under untruncated softmax.

Returning to unit temperature and applying nucleus truncation with \(\NucleusProbabilityMass =\) 0.90 evaluates cumulative probability mass. The lead token \(\VocabularyToken_1\) provides 0.7840, which falls short of the threshold. Incorporating \(\VocabularyToken_2\) (0.1749) brings cumulative coverage to 0.9589, which satisfies the threshold and defines the active nucleus as \(\{\VocabularyToken_1, \VocabularyToken_2\}\). Renormalizing across this active set assigns probability 0.8176 to \(\VocabularyToken_1\) and 0.1824 to \(\VocabularyToken_2\), while strictly zeroing the remaining tail tokens \(\{\VocabularyToken_3, \VocabularyToken_4, \VocabularyToken_5\}\).

Systems insight: Temperature governs candidate diversity. Halving temperature compresses the non-top probability tail from 21.6 percent to 5.0 percent, achieving a 4.3× reduction, whereas nucleus truncation eliminates the tail entirely. Repeated calls produce diverse outputs only when probability mass occupies this tail, which is the stochastic resource that parallel candidate sampling draws on in Parallel sampling along the breadth axis. Because neither transformation ensures that the highest-scoring candidate is correct, engineering teams configure decoding parameters based on empirical task pass rates rather than raw model likelihood.

2.4.3 Repetition and replay

Greedy decoding removes sampling variance from a fixed logit vector, but it does not guarantee end-to-end reproducibility across served API calls. In distributed production infrastructure, identical input requests frequently encounter different serving nodes, hardware accelerator revisions, or software runtime configurations. Subtle variations in floating-point reduction order6 can shift closely contested logits in their least significant bits; flipping an argmax selection at position \(\TokenIndex=1\) permanently alters all downstream conditioning. Similarly, configuring a fixed pseudorandom seed constrains the sampling generator without eliminating numerical non-determinism across backend kernels or cluster deployments. True reproducibility requires a strictly pinned execution environment and software contract, extending well beyond setting \(\SamplingTemperature = 0\). Real-world production incidents illustrate how infrastructure changes in the serving fleet can dramatically alter generation behavior while caller requests remain identical.

6 Floating-Point Reduction Order: Floating-point addition lacks associativity because intermediate calculations undergo rounding at each operation (Goldberg 1991). Parallel attention and reduction kernels that accumulate dot products across differing thread block dimensions or dynamic batch sizes execute summation in varying orders, yielding minor discrepancies in the least significant bits of logit tensors. When two top candidates have nearly identical values, this numerical divergence suffices to invert a greedy selection, redirecting the subsequent autoregressive path.

Goldberg, David. 1991. “What Every Computer Scientist Should Know about Floating-Point Arithmetic.” ACM Computing Surveys 23 (1): 5–48. https://doi.org/10.1145/103162.103163.

War Story 2.1: Three serving bugs behind unchanged requests (2025)
Context: Between August and early September 2025, three separate infrastructure bugs intermittently degraded the responses of Anthropic’s Claude models (Anthropic 2025). These defects stemmed entirely from the serving infrastructure rather than caller requests or model weights.

Mechanism: A front-end routing defect directed standard traffic to server clusters configured for extended context windows, while session stickiness (routing a client’s follow-up requests to the server that handled its earlier ones) kept those follow-up requests on the misconfigured nodes. Concurrently, an improperly deployed kernel optimization on a specialized accelerator cluster occasionally assigned outsized probability to improbable tokens. Finally, an optimization in the token-selection pipeline triggered a compiler bug: an approximate top-\(\TopTokenSampleCount\) routine with mixed-precision subroutines emitted incorrect tokens under specific dynamic batch sizes.

Impact: During peak impact, the routing failure corrupted 16 percent of requests to one production model, and approximately 30 percent of active coding agent users encountered at least one misrouted interaction. Generated responses exhibited foreign-language script insertions within English prose and syntax anomalies in generated source code. Automated regression suites initially missed these failures because a response often recovered from an isolated corrupted token later in generation, and intermittent runtime conditions meant an identical prompt could succeed on one invocation and fail catastrophically on the next.

Fix: Engineers rolled back the offending kernel deployments, corrected cluster routing rules, and replaced the approximate top-\(\TopTokenSampleCount\) kernel with an exact selection routine running in 32-bit floating-point precision, trading a minor throughput penalty for numerical stability. Anthropic subsequently deployed continuous canary evaluations across production fleets to catch subtle generational drift.

Systems lesson: Output distributions reflect the concrete serving pipeline just as heavily as the model weights or input context. Corrupted tokens arrived packaged in well-formed HTTP payloads and cleanly closed generation blocks, a direct instance of the fail-plausible fault (The fail-plausible fault). Robust agent architectures must log full call-level metadata (runtime versions, routing tags, and sampling parameters) while subjecting every proposed action to external environment validation regardless of nominal generation success.

Anthropic. 2025. A Postmortem of Three Recent Issues. Anthropic engineering incident report.

Production failures of this nature define the baseline instrumentation required for agentic infrastructure. Comprehensive diagnostic logs must capture the exact prompt payload, model and tokenizer revision hashes, decoding hyperparameters, configured token limits, and any backend routing or seed metadata. Alongside input parameters, the system must record the raw response payload, the explicit finish reason, and token usage metrics. When diagnosing downstream agent failures, developers should distinguish between replaying a recorded trajectory and re-querying the model. Replaying a recorded trace isolates downstream execution logic from model nondeterminism, whereas issuing a repeated API call tests the resilience of the agent across sampling variations. Rigorous evaluation frameworks must measure and report this repeated-call variance rather than assuming consistent single-call execution.

An agent runtime therefore receives candidate tokens produced under a specific decoding configuration and ended by a specific stop condition. That stop condition is the first thing the runtime must read. A generation that ran into its output ceiling, was refused, or was cut off in transit can still deliver bytes that look like a finished proposal, and no check on the payload alone tells the difference. Before asking whether a read_file proposal names an existing, permitted path, the runtime needs a record of whether a complete candidate arrived at all.

2.5 The Invocation Contract

Suppose a model begins generating a cleanup command targeting the path /tmp/scratch/cache_dir, but reaches its token limit immediately after emitting /tmp/scratch. Both strings form syntactically valid shell commands, yet they specify vastly different deletion boundaries. An execution engine inspecting only raw text cannot determine whether the shorter path represents an intended command or a dangerously truncated prefix. To prevent catastrophic side effects, the host runtime requires an explicit execution outcome alongside the payload.

By pairing request specifications with normalized response envelopes at the invocation boundary, the runtime treats each remote generation as a unified contract.

Definition 2.2: Model invocation contract

Model invocation contract is the pair of records that bounds one model call: a request fixing the input, model configuration, sampling settings, and enforced output and time limits, and a normalized envelope reporting the call’s status, payload, and resource use.

  1. Significance: The contract establishes each model call as a distinct, countable unit within an agent trajectory. Its usage record prices the call’s share of \(\AttemptCost\), and its status flag isolates completed generations from partial or failed calls. Consequently, the cost per accepted task (equation 1.2) can be computed from recorded telemetry rather than heuristically reconstructed from conversation transcripts.
  2. Distinction: The invocation contract describes the model call rather than the overarching task. A generated tool request can fully conform to decoding limits without ever being dispatched, and a fully generated response can fail downstream validation. Dispatch authorization, tool execution, and task-level acceptance remain separate runtime decisions backed by their own evidence.
  3. Common pitfall: Recording only the payload text. Without explicit status flags and usage accounting, a truncated fragment is indistinguishable from an intentional short response, and unrecorded token consumption silently distorts operational cost accounting as zero-cost execution.

2.5.1 Formal request specification

At the generation interface, the bounded request is represented by the tuple

\[\ModelCallRequest = \langle \RequestPromptTokens, \text{ModelID}, \OutputLimit, \CompletionDeadline, \StopSequenceSet, \SamplingParameters \rangle\]

The prompt tokens \(\RequestPromptTokens = (\PromptToken_1, \PromptToken_2, \dots, \PromptToken_{\SequenceLength})\) encode the serialized messages, role markers, advertised tool schemas, and formatting delimiters visible to the model. The runtime constructs this sequence from structured messages and tools configuration fields prior to serialization.

ModelID identifies the target model weights and serving deployment requested through model_config. Systems engineers must distinguish mutable provider aliases (such as model-latest) from immutable, pinned model revisions (such as content-addressed snapshots). The sampling parameters \(\SamplingParameters\), including temperature and top-\(\NucleusProbabilityMass\), configure the stochastic selection rule applied during autoregressive decode. Together with the serialized input and returned output, the resolved configuration uniquely explains a call; exact regeneration also depends on backend serving implementation details, as war story 2.1 demonstrated.

The resource limits specify an output allowance \(\OutputLimit\) and a local waiting deadline \(\CompletionDeadline\). Systems cannot rely on the model itself to halt generation gracefully. Invariant closure (principle 1) places these limits below the model, so the host runtime and the remote serving system enforce them mechanically. The runtime verifies that the combined prompt and generation budget fits within the service engine’s context window. In addition, the adapter records whether the provider charges non-visible reasoning tokens against \(\OutputLimit\), since measuring visible text length alone would understate actual resource consumption.

The stop sequences \(\StopSequenceSet\) define token patterns that trigger an immediate halt at the serving interface. When an inference engine encounters a matching sequence, it stops decoding according to provider matching semantics. However, an engine matches byte sequences blindly: encountering a stop sequence inside an argument or quoted string can terminate generation before closing brackets or quotation marks are emitted. The host runtime must therefore record the provider’s raw stop reason and validate candidate syntax independently.

Resource enforcement operates across two distinct physical boundaries: the client host and the remote serving cluster. On the host side, the runtime enforces a receive buffer limit on incoming bytes and a local deadline \(\CompletionDeadline\) on waiting time. An expired local deadline halts host waiting and marks the invocation as failed, but it provides no guarantee that the remote server has halted execution or freed accelerator memory.7 Server-side token ceilings and cancellation protocols complement these client-side controls. Every operational limit must clearly specify which component enforces it and which physical resource it governs.

7 Remote Call Semantics: Remote procedure call designers met this ambiguity first. In the design Birrell and Nelson (1984) describe, a call that returns normally executed exactly once, but a call that ends in a failure report may have executed once or not at all, because the caller cannot tell a lost request from a lost reply. A model call inherits the same gap: a timed-out generation may still have run to completion and been billed, which is why the normalized status for a failed delivery, TRANSPORT_FAILURE (table 2), records an unknown remote outcome rather than a call that never happened.

Birrell, Andrew D., and Bruce Jay Nelson. 1984. “Implementing Remote Procedure Calls.” ACM Transactions on Computer Systems 2 (1): 39–59. https://doi.org/10.1145/2080.357392.

2.5.2 The normalized status envelope

The runtime represents the observed outcome using the normalized status envelope

\[\NormalizedStatusEnvelope = \langle \text{status}, \ResponsePayload, \ResponseMetadata \rangle\]

Here, \(\ResponsePayload\) denotes the returned payload, which may be complete, partial, or absent. The metadata record \(\ResponseMetadata\) captures operational telemetry: the response kind, the raw provider stop_reason, reported token usage, unique request identifiers, latency measurements, and serving parameters. In concrete implementations, these attributes appear as fields on a response object; grouping them formally into a tuple decouples the raw text payload from the evidence documenting how it arrived. Unreported usage represents unmeasured cost rather than zero cost.

Because proprietary model APIs express termination conditions using divergent finish reasons, error codes, and transport exceptions, the adapter maps provider-specific signals onto the seven normalized statuses defined in table 2. This taxonomy provides this book’s normalized interface. Two of the statuses, REJECTED and CANCELED, arise only while a response is still arriving; section 2.5.3 develops the stream monitor that produces them. A status flag records the outcome verified locally by the host runtime; when concurrent events race, such as a local cancellation overlapping an incoming completion frame, the adapter applies deterministic resolution rules while preserving the raw provider signals in \(\ResponseMetadata\).

For an artifact such as a read_file request, kind="tool_request" instructs the runtime which candidate representation to validate, while status="COMPLETED" confirms that generation finished under the interface contract. Neither field confirms that the host dispatched the tool or that the filesystem operation succeeded; completion tracks protocol generation, whereas execution and task acceptance remain separate host decisions.

Applying invariant closure (principle 1) to the status field establishes a mandatory gate: the quarantining rule. Under this rule, any payload whose invocation envelope reports a status other than COMPLETED cannot pass into the candidate execution pipeline. The runtime must verify the status flag before admitting content to downstream parsing or action dispatch. While the system may inspect malformed or interrupted output inside an isolated diagnostic sandbox, that inspection cannot authorize tool dispatch or silently reclassify the fragment as a finished proposal. When later chapters sample several candidates for one task, a candidate cut off by a token limit obeys the same rule. Under the quarantining rule, a truncated payload is treated as a diagnosable failure rather than a partial action.

The cleanup command introduced earlier demonstrates why structural parsing alone cannot replace this status check. Suppose the complete intended command is

rm -rf /tmp/scratch/cache_dir

If an exhausted token quota truncates generation midway through the path, the resulting prefix is

rm -rf /tmp/scratch

The truncated string parses as valid shell syntax, but it broadens the deletion scope to the entire parent directory. If the envelope marks this output as TRUNCATED, the quarantining rule holds the command in quarantine, preventing accidental execution. Conversely, receiving status="COMPLETED" does not grant execution authority; it merely establishes that the model finished generating its proposal. Neither command represents an approved cleanup policy on its own; the comparison underscores that syntactic validity cannot substitute for verified completion evidence.

Listing 3 implements this admission check. The gate admits only completed generations to downstream processing, leaving syntax validation, permission checks, and task-level verification to subsequent stages.

Incomplete payloads are redirected to a dedicated diagnostic log governed by its own memory and access bounds. This log retains the partial byte stream alongside the observed stop_reason to assist failure analysis. Attempting to “repair” a truncated fragment by appending guessed arguments or synthetic delimiters produces an entirely new candidate devoid of generation provenance; such synthetic patches can never inherit the COMPLETED status of the original call.

2.5.3 Streaming and cancellation

Streaming delivers incremental response fragments across an active network connection before generation finishes. A delivery chunk can contain part of a decoded sub-word, several tokens, or an application-layer framing event. The host runtime must adhere to the provider’s framing protocol rather than assuming a one-to-one correspondence between chunks and tokens. Incremental inspection allows the runtime to discover early structural violations; if a prefix violates format constraints, the monitor halts waiting immediately rather than buffering unusable tokens. Because generation is ongoing, the monitor evaluates whether the prefix can transition to a valid completion, rather than testing whether the prefix is already complete.

Rejection and cancellation represent distinct operational events. When an incremental monitor detects a structural violation, the host runtime sets the call status to REJECTED and issues a cancellation signal to the remote endpoint. If an operator or runtime policy cancels an otherwise compliant response before accepting completion, the runtime marks the status as CANCELED. A local deadline expiry without accepted completion instead maps to TRANSPORT_FAILURE, as in table 2; the runtime may then request cancellation while tracking remote termination separately. Withholding the partial payload from tool dispatch is an invariant the host enforces locally in each case. However, halting remote decode loops and reclaiming accelerator memory require the serving engine to process and honor the cancellation request. Simply closing a TCP socket provides no guarantee that remote execution has ceased.

Consider a call with an output allowance of 4,096 tokens whose stream validator detects a schema violation at step \(\RejectionDecodeStep =\) 96. The runtime’s part of the response finishes at once. It records the rejection, discards the partial proposal, and sends a cancel request. The service’s part depends on whether that request is honored (figure 3).

The skipped-step count shown is an upper bound that requires provider cooperation. If the serving engine processes the cancellation promptly, it skips up to \(\OutputLimit - \RejectionDecodeStep =\) 4,000 autoregressive decode steps. In the reference unbatched deployment, each plain autoregressive decode step reads the model weights from accelerator memory, so the step takes at least the weight bytes divided by the memory bandwidth; for the reference deployment of section 2.8, that floor is 21.075 ms per generated token. At that reference floor, skipping all these steps avoids 84.3 s of estimated decode work. An honored cancellation also frees the request’s slot among the requests the serving engine decodes together and releases the call’s KV-cache allocations. If the remote service fails to honor the cancellation, the host runtime still quarantines the rejected payload locally, but the remote service may continue generating up to its token allowance.

Nonstreaming invocations enforce the same admission barrier once the whole response arrives. Streaming moves the observation boundary earlier in the call, enabling rapid rejection and resource recovery without relaxing the rule that incomplete prefixes never trigger external side effects. Either way, the status gate settles only whether a complete candidate exists. A COMPLETED read_file proposal can still name a missing file or a path outside the workspace, so the next question is which property each subsequent check on that candidate can establish.

2.6 Candidate Sequence Verification

Suppose the status gate has admitted a completed read_file proposal with path set to src/pool.py. Completion says only that generation finished, and, as the validation and authorization stages in section 2.2.2 showed, schema conformance says nothing about whether the file exists or lies inside the workspace. Generative inference produces a candidate token sequence; neither high statistical likelihood nor schema conformance determines whether the runtime environment can safely act on it.

The same distinction applies when the model emits a source code patch rather than an inspection request. A candidate patch can parse cleanly, compile against existing headers, and pass a sequential unit test while leaving the underlying race condition untouched. Each verification check answers a narrow, specific question rather than certifying overall task satisfaction. Robust candidate verification systems must track these separate dimensions of evidence explicitly rather than collapsing them into a single, undifferentiated notion of validity.

2.6.1 Likelihood and checked properties

Autoregressive generation assigns probabilities based on patterns in training data and context tokens, reflecting linguistic familiarity rather than operational truth. Whether a generated path exists on disk, whether the caller holds read permissions, and whether an altered function preserves system invariants depend entirely on the operational environment and the task contract. An obsolete or nonexistent path often receives substantial model probability simply because it matches common directory conventions in the training corpus.

Sound verification begins by pairing each concrete property with the precise evidence required to validate it. For a read_file invocation, a schema validator checks that the payload contains only supported keys and provides a string-valued path. A passing result proves syntactic schema membership, provided the validator implements the schema dialect faithfully and inspects the exact object forwarded to execution. Proving that the target file exists requires probing the filesystem, while establishing access rights requires evaluating the task’s current capability grant against host operating system policies.

Static analysis and dynamic testing support fundamentally different operational claims. A compiler verifies type consistency and language constraints under fixed configuration flags, whereas a linter detects anti-patterns through static heuristic rules. Dynamic test suites execute concrete code paths and record runtime assertions, so a passing single-threaded checkout test says nothing about the connection pool under concurrent contention.

↩︎ The closure evidence levels, from self-report and static checks through visible and sealed tests to formal proof, are established in The invariant closure principle.

8 Complete Mediation: One of the design principles that Saltzer and Schroeder (1975) set out for protection systems: every access to every object is checked against current authority at the moment of access, not remembered from an earlier decision. The tool handler applies the same rule to a model’s proposal, so a path validated when the request was composed is checked again when the read executes, closing the gap in which the grant or the file could have changed.

Saltzer, J. H., and M. D. Schroeder. 1975. “The Protection of Information in Computer Systems.” Proceedings of the IEEE 63 (9): 1278–308. https://doi.org/10.1109/proc.1975.9939.

Architecturally, these boundaries require the execution runtime to treat generated model output strictly as untrusted data rather than direct control flow. A candidate tool call remains passive data until an authorized tool handler ingests, verifies, and dispatches it. That handler enforces access boundaries at execution time,8 completely independent of any self-assertions the model makes regarding its own permissions. Similarly, evaluating an untrusted patch requires running arbitrary machine instructions, demanding isolated execution sandboxes even during preliminary test runs.

2.6.2 Self-review and external evidence

Prompts that instruct a model to inspect or refine its own patch can surface shallow syntax errors or expose omitted edge cases. Model-based self-review, however, is simply another autoregressive generation step sampled from the same parameter distribution and conditioned on the same incomplete context. When the generator operates under an incorrect assumption about an API or concurrency model, a self-review prompt typically reinforces that assumption rather than exposing it. Re-prompting does not create statistical independence. Empirical evaluations of self-correction without external grounding demonstrate this limit clearly: across complex reasoning tasks, models prompted to inspect their own answers rarely improve their accuracy and frequently introduce regressions (Huang et al. 2024).

Huang, Jie, Xinyun Chen, Swaroop Mishra, Huaixiu Steven Zheng, Adams Wei Yu, Xinying Song, and Denny Zhou. 2024. “Large Language Models Cannot Self-Correct Reasoning Yet.” International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).
Zheng, Lianmin, Wei-Lin Chiang, Ying Sheng, Siyuan Zhuang, Hao Wu, Yonghao Zhuang, Zi Lin, et al. 2023. “Judging LLM-as-a-Judge with MT-Bench and Chatbot Arena.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 36: 46595–623.

Engineers must maintain a firm conceptual separation between candidate refinement and formal property certification. A model-generated critique can guide iterative search, but external tools (compilers, linters, sandboxed test harnesses, and kernel access-control checks) must provide evidence for the specific properties they check. No critique emitted by a model, whether self-generated or evaluated by an auxiliary judge model, carries the authority to validate a security boundary or approve deployment. When a system does use a learned judge (a second model prompted or trained to grade the first model’s output) as a secondary evaluator, its verdicts must be treated as noisy signals with measurable false-positive rates and known systematic biases, such as preferences for verbose explanations or specific candidate positions (Zheng et al. 2023).

A conditional-probability calculation clarifies why fallible verification gates leave residual risk. The following model parameters reflect a hypothetical scenario evaluating a candidate patch against a concurrency specification:

Systems Perspective 2.1: What a passing verdict leaves unresolved
Let \(\CandidateDefectRate\) denote the defect rate, the share of generated patches that violate a specified behavioral property (a local symbol here, distinct from the per-turn error rate of horizon compounding), and let \(\VerifierFalseAcceptanceRate\) denote the checker’s false-accept rate, the probability that it passes a defective patch. Suppose \(\CandidateDefectRate =\) 18 percent, \(\VerifierFalseAcceptanceRate =\) 0.65, and the checker passes valid patches with probability 0.85. Bayes’ rule gives the fraction of defective patches among the passing results:

\[\Pr(\text{Defective} \mid \text{PASS}) = \frac{\VerifierFalseAcceptanceRate \cdot \CandidateDefectRate}{\VerifierFalseAcceptanceRate \cdot \CandidateDefectRate + \Pr(\text{PASS} \mid \text{Valid}) \cdot (1 - \CandidateDefectRate)} \tag{4}\]

Substituting the assumed rates gives \(\frac{0.65 \times 0.18}{(0.65 \times 0.18) + (0.85 \times (1 - 0.18))}\approx 14.37\%\). The defect share among passing patches falls only from 18 percent to 14.37 percent, because the checker rejects 35 percent of defective patches while also rejecting 15 percent of valid ones. The lever is \(\VerifierFalseAcceptanceRate\): a checker with half the false-accept rate, 0.325, lowers the residual to 7.7 percent. Repeating the same check buys no such guarantee, because its behavior on the surviving candidates is correlated with the first pass. Every defective patch that passes also inflates the apparent acceptance probability \(\AcceptanceProbability\) in the cost per accepted task (equation 1.2).

A sound checker for the exact property drives \(\VerifierFalseAcceptanceRate\) to zero under its assumptions. Determinism alone does not supply soundness, and soundness for schema membership does not extend to patch behavior. The residual in equation 4 is one minus the precision \(\VerifierPrecision\) of a passing verdict in the verification asymmetry (principle 3), written there with valid-candidate rate \(\ValidCandidateRate = 1 - \CandidateDefectRate\) and false-reject rate \(\VerifierFalseRejectionRate = 1 - \Pr(\text{PASS} \mid \text{Valid})\). That principle holds that producing a candidate, selecting it, and establishing that it satisfies the task are different events, and it yields a design rule. Select each validation gate for the specific property it establishes and its false-accept rate, and let the task contract determine what residual defect rate the system can tolerate.

2.6.3 A pipeline of scoped checks

In production systems, executing full end-to-end integration tests or sandboxed reproduction scripts is orders of magnitude more expensive than parsing JSON or running a static linter. Consequently, the runtime organizes candidate verification into an ascending cascade, rejecting malformed or unauthorized requests early before spending compute on deep behavioral evaluation. Staging checks by cost optimizes throughput, but each gate validates an orthogonal property rather than an increasingly strict approximation of overall correctness. Table 3 categorizes these verification layers alongside their computational overhead and formal evidentiary boundaries.

Consider how this tiered pipeline evaluates an inspection request. Behind the status gate of section 2.5, a read_file proposal needs only the first two rows of table 3 before dispatch. Executing the read operation simply feeds environmental state back to the agent for subsequent reasoning cycles rather than completing the overall bug fix. Conversely, when evaluating a proposed patch, the pipeline validates patch syntax and file targets, applies the diff inside an isolated scratch workspace, and triggers targeted unit tests. Final acceptance adheres to the task contract’s explicit evidentiary standard, escalating to human review when automated checks cannot decisively settle safety or correctness criteria.

The verification cascade inspects output the model has already generated. A streaming monitor can reject an invalid prefix before the response finishes, but the offending token has already been emitted, and recovering still costs a fresh call. When the required output structure is known before the call, the syntax constraint can move inside generation and prevent the malformed token instead of rejecting it after emission.

2.7 Grammar-Guided Decoding

Consider a software repair agent attempting to inspect an unfamiliar module by calling read_file(path) with argument "src/pool.py". If the model’s generated text omits the closing quotation mark before emitting an end-of-turn token, the runtime parser encounters malformed JSON and rejects the payload before dispatching the call. That parser check shields the execution boundary from syntactic corruption, yet resolving the failure demands another full call: queueing, prefill, and every decode step again, with a fresh chance of a different error. When the system knows the target output structure ahead of time, the inference engine can instead remove the tokens that would violate the required syntax during generation, so the defect never reaches the tool boundary.

Definition 2.3: Grammar-constrained decoding

Grammar-constrained decoding restricts next-token selection during autoregressive generation to tokens whose decoded text preserves a viable prefix of a target formal grammar.

  1. Significance: Dynamic token masking guarantees that any completed generation sequence strictly satisfies the structural grammar, eliminating syntax errors at the model boundary when the constraint parser covers the target schema.
  2. Distinction: Post-generation validation inspects an already emitted sequence and discards invalid payloads after full generation. Constrained decoding actively shapes the vocabulary distribution at every generation step. Even so, neither approach verifies whether a syntactically correct payload contains sensible parameter values or possesses valid execution permissions.
  3. Common pitfall: Mistaking a viable prefix for a completed data structure. If token limits, request timeouts, or connection failures truncate generation early, the emitted sequence remains an unfinished prefix that cannot be safely dispatched.

2.7.1 Legal prefixes and completed structures

The constraint engine maintains a running parse state synchronized with the sequence generated so far. Consider a schema expecting a string-valued path parameter. Once the prefix {"path": appears in the decoding buffer, the grammar allows optional whitespace followed by an opening quotation mark, but forbids unquoted booleans, numbers, or bare identifiers. When the generator emits {"path":"src/, the text represents a viable prefix:9 while incomplete as a JSON document, it can still transition into a valid object through subsequent string and delimiter tokens. The fundamental mechanism of constrained decoding relies on evaluating whether a candidate token can extend the current prefix toward an accepting grammar state.

9 Legal (Viable) Prefix: The viable-prefix concept stems from classical compiler theory. The LR parsers introduced by Knuth (1965) read input strings left to right, identifying an error at the exact first symbol that cannot extend the sequence into any valid sentence of the language. A grammar mask applies this principle constructively: instead of catching syntax violations after generation finishes, the engine prunes invalid transition tokens from the vocabulary before sampling.

Knuth, Donald E. 1965. “On the Translation of Languages from Left to Right.” Information and Control 8 (6): 607–39. https://doi.org/10.1016/S0019-9958(65)90426-2.

At generation step \(\TokenIndex\), let \(\DecodingConstraintState_t\) represent the parser’s internal state, including nested scope tracking for recursive structures. Let \(\ValidTokenSet(\DecodingConstraintState_t)\) denote the set of vocabulary tokens whose decoded strings maintain a viable prefix under the target grammar. Because modern language models operate on subword tokens rather than individual characters, a single token can span multiple characters, punctuation marks, or whitespace segments (for example, a single token .py"} that finishes the extension, closes the string, and closes the object). The engine must therefore simulate the parser across the token’s full decoded byte sequence to confirm that every character transition is valid.

Incremental parsing demonstrates that pruning invalid tokens at each decoding step produces syntactically flawless outputs for domain-specific languages such as SQL (Scholak et al. 2021). To minimize per-step latency, guided-generation frameworks precompute transition tables between the target grammar and the model vocabulary, converting the valid-token search into an efficient table lookup during the forward pass (Willard and Louf 2023).

For each token index \(\MaskTokenIndex\) in vocabulary \(\TokenVocabulary\), the constraint engine constructs an additive logit mask:

\[\LogitMaskEntry_i(\DecodingConstraintState_t) = \begin{cases} 0 & \text{if } \MaskTokenIndex \in \ValidTokenSet(\DecodingConstraintState_t) \\ -\infty & \text{if } \MaskTokenIndex \notin \ValidTokenSet(\DecodingConstraintState_t) \end{cases}\]

Adding this mask to the unnormalized logit vector \(\LogitVector_t\) produces the filtered logit vector \(\MaskedLogitVector_t = \LogitVector_t + \LogitMask(\DecodingConstraintState_t)\). At unit temperature, the resulting softmax distribution becomes

\[\SoftmaxOutputProbability_{t, i} = \frac{\exp(\MaskedTokenLogit_{t, i})}{\sum_{j \in \TokenVocabulary} \exp(\MaskedTokenLogit_{t, j})} = \begin{cases} \frac{\exp(\OutputLogit_{t, i})}{\sum_{k \in \ValidTokenSet(\DecodingConstraintState_t)} \exp(\OutputLogit_{t, k})} & \text{if } \MaskTokenIndex \in \ValidTokenSet(\DecodingConstraintState_t) \\ 0 & \text{if } \MaskTokenIndex \notin \ValidTokenSet(\DecodingConstraintState_t) \end{cases} \tag{5}\]

Setting the logits of invalid tokens to \(-\infty\) forces their sampling probabilities to zero, renormalizing the distribution entirely across viable continuations. Once the sampler draws a token from this restricted set, the engine consumes that token’s characters and advances the parser to state \(\DecodingConstraintState_{t+1}\). This formulation assumes that \(\ValidTokenSet(\DecodingConstraintState_t)\) remains nonempty and that the sampling configuration respects the mask. The engine applies the grammar mask before top-\(\TopTokenSampleCount\) or nucleus truncation so that these filters select among legal continuations. If an implementation instead truncates support first and no retained token belongs to \(\ValidTokenSet(\DecodingConstraintState_t)\), the serving runtime must raise an explicit constraint-satisfaction error rather than silently disabling the mask. Positive finite temperature rescales the remaining probabilities without removing their support.

Recomputing \(\ValidTokenSet(\DecodingConstraintState_t)\) from scratch at every step would cost a scan of the whole vocabulary per token. Engines therefore compile the tool schema once into an automaton whose states each carry a precomputed mask \(\LogitMask(\DecodingConstraintState_t)\), so that each step is a lookup followed by a state transition. The automaton in figure 4 is compiled for a query tool with two fields, query and limit, rather than for read_file; the mechanism is identical.

Generating viable prefixes guarantees that the output will not contain invalid syntax, but it does not guarantee that generation finishes cleanly. For the output to form a complete, usable payload, two operational conditions must hold: the parser must reach an accepting state, and the generation sequence must terminate according to the protocol rules. The constraint engine must disallow end-of-sequence (<eos>) tokens whenever required fields or closing delimiters remain unwritten. As a result, maintaining viable prefixes provides a conditional guarantee: completed outputs are structurally sound, but the system cannot assume that every generation request finishes within its allocated token budget.

↪ The automaton behind the token mask, and the induction showing that a constrained output ending in a permitted end token belongs to the grammar, are developed in Finite-state constrained decoding.

Consider what happens when an inference request hits its maximum output token limit while emitting arguments, leaving the decoding buffer at {"path":"src/po. The string is a viable prefix under the schema, yet the envelope reports status: TRUNCATED, and the quarantining rule of section 2.5.2 applies exactly as it did to the shortened rm -rf path.
