A heuristic repair parser that appends the closing quote and brace would fabricate the call read_file("src/po"), which the model never finished and which names a file nobody asked for. The mask governs which tokens can appear; it never promotes an interrupted generation to a completed one.

2.7.2 Scope of the structural guarantee

Grammar masking moves the first row of table 3 into generation and leaves every other row where it was. A masked {"path":"src/pool.py"} still names a file that may not exist or may lie outside the grant, because the mask operates strictly on the textual representation and never queries repository state or security policy. Property by property, the mask’s own reach is narrow (table 4).

The strength of the structural guarantee depends directly on the formal expressive power of the enforced grammar. Enforcing a general JSON grammar guarantees only that the output obeys RFC 8259 syntax (balanced braces, legal numeric formats, and escaped strings) without constraining object keys or data types. In contrast, compiling a specific JSON Schema into the constraint engine allows the runtime to enforce required properties, restrict types (such as distinguishing floating-point numbers from integers), and constrain string values to specific regular expressions or enumerations.

Engineering challenges arise when a schema relies on advanced features that the constraint compiler cannot translate into finite-state or pushdown automata. For example, schema keywords enforcing cross-field conditional dependencies (if/then/else), unique array elements, or complex lookahead assertions often exceed the capabilities of precomputed token masks. If an execution engine encounters unsupported schema features, an improper grammar compiler, or divergent type interpretations between the generator and consumer, the structural guarantee collapses. To maintain system integrity, the runtime must document which schema features its engine enforces and perform a final validation pass against its authoritative interface before dispatch. Logit masking acts as a mechanical filter situated below the model in the hierarchy of invariant closure (principle 1): it guarantees syntactic conformance without requiring model cooperation, leaving semantic acceptance and authorization to verification layers situated above the model.

Schema structure directly influences how an agent handles ambiguity or missing context. When a schema strictly mandates a file path with no alternative representations, every accepted completion must provide a path string, even when the prompt context provides no reasonable clue about which file to inspect. By stripping away all tokens outside the target structure, an over-constrained mask removes the agent’s ability to communicate that critical information is missing. Under these conditions, the model has no choice but to invent a plausible file path to reach an accepting parser state.

Systems engineers can prevent this forced confabulation by designing schemas that incorporate explicit fallback variants, such as an unknown payload variant or a structured error field. Providing an unknown branch allows the agent to signal insufficient information directly within the schema without inventing fictitious data. These variants represent distinct semantic branches within the model’s output payload; they should not be confused with transport-level invocation statuses or runtime tool execution failures.

Engineers must also recognize that introducing fallback branches does not prevent semantic misuse. An agent might still output a fabricated file path when an unknown variant is available, or falsely trigger an error state when sufficient context exists. The execution runtime must evaluate each payload variant according to the application’s task contract, gathering targeted observations or asking for user clarification when uncertainty arises. The schema provides the expressiveness necessary for honest reporting; external system observations determine whether the agent’s reported choice is justified.

2.7.3 Learned formatting and runtime constraints

Supervised fine-tuning (further training on curated example responses, developed in Trajectory Fine-Tuning) and instruction tuning teach models to associate natural language prompts with specific tool definitions, biasing generation toward well-structured function arguments (Schick et al. 2023). Runtime logit masking operates at a different systems layer: it determines the exact mathematical support of the vocabulary distribution at each generation step. Learned formatting shifts probability mass toward expected syntax, while masking reduces the probability of every illegal token strictly to zero. These two strategies reinforce each other. A model tuned for structured calling reliably allocates high logits to semantically appropriate tokens, while the runtime mask guarantees that subtle sampling variations or long-context degradations cannot produce malformed syntax.

Schick, Timo, Jane Dwivedi-Yu, Roberto Dessı̀, Roberta Raileanu, Maria Lomeli, Luke Zettlemoyer, Nicola Cancedda, and Thomas Scialom. 2023. “Toolformer: Language Models Can Teach Themselves to Use Tools.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 36: 68539–51.

Neither approach substitutes for the other. Statistical alignment alone yields no formal guarantee against syntax violations, while a grammar mask contributes zero domain knowledge toward selecting the correct file path. The overall reliability of the agent depends on the synergy between training fidelity, expressive schema definitions, and robust runtime constraints.

Furthermore, deploying grammar constraints introduces concrete systems trade-offs that engineers must measure across the end-to-end request lifecycle. Compiling complex regular expressions or nested JSON schemas into deterministic automata consumes CPU time and memory. In dynamic environments where agents construct ad hoc schemas per request, compilation latency can offset the latency saved by avoiding syntax retries. Similarly, tracking parse states and computing masks across large vocabularies (\(|\TokenVocabulary| \ge 128{,}000\) tokens) adds per-token decoding overhead unless supported by precomputed lookup indices or bitmasks applied on the accelerator. A systems engineer must evaluate compilation cost, serving memory footprint, and decoding throughput together, rather than assuming that eliminating syntax retries automatically reduces total operational cost.

Checkpoint 2.2: Evaluating schema-constrained decoding

A repair agent uses constrained decoding for file-reading proposals. Its runtime records response kind and invocation status separately.

Constrained decoding eliminates an entire class of syntax failures before dispatch, but a structurally valid invocation still requires sufficient compute budget to reach completion and external empirical evidence to justify execution. An agent architecture must account for schema compilation, input prompt processing, and token generation as distinct latency and cost factors. Balancing these overheads against the risk of unconstrained retries determines whether grammar-guided decoding delivers a net performance and reliability gain for a given task.

2.8 The Cost of a Model Call

When an automated repair agent requests a code fix, it can ask the model for a localized diff or for the entire modified file. Both formats describe the same underlying patch, but the full-file approach forces the model to regenerate hundreds of lines of unchanged syntax alongside the edit. The runtime absorbs the latency and compute cost of generating those redundant tokens long before a compiler or test runner can validate the candidate patch. An effective invocation contract must therefore budget prompt reading and output writing explicitly, rather than treating every model invocation as a uniform, black-box unit of work.

Input length and output length stress distinct subsystems in the inference serving stack. Prompt ingestion exposes many known token positions to parallel matrix computation across the accelerator’s execution units. Conversely, autoregressive token generation executes as a strictly sequential loop, where each new token depends on the hidden state of all preceding positions. Because hardware architectures handle these two phases under vastly different physical constraints, output length serves as the primary software lever for controlling generation latency. However, an excessively large prompt, an undersized reply, or a congested service queue can quickly shift the latency bottleneck elsewhere.

2.8.1 Call latency decomposition

From the perspective of an agent runtime, a model invocation begins when the runtime prepares the outbound request and ends when the normalized response is ready for dispatch to a tool or validation check. Within this end-to-end boundary, preparation spans local context assembly, payload serialization, and network dispatch; queueing measures the idle wait in the serving system’s admission queue; prefill processes the supplied prompt tokens in a single parallel forward pass; decode generates completion tokens through an autoregressive sequence of forward passes; and postprocessing encompasses network response transfer, wire deserialization, and schema validation. A non-overlapping accounting of these phases yields

\[\CallLatency = \CallPreparationTime + \RequestQueueTime + \PrefillTime + \DecodeInterval + \CallPostprocessingTime \tag{6}\]

Each term in equation 6 represents an elapsed duration in seconds, captured via high-resolution timestamps rather than derived backward from token counts. When preparation or network transfer overlaps with server-side execution, telemetry spans must track concurrent execution explicitly to avoid double-counting. An agent runtime that instruments only aggregate wall-clock duration cannot diagnose whether an unexpected latency spike stems from a bloated prompt, a saturated inference queue, or an excessively verbose generation, and each of those causes demands a different fix.

Accounting for the first generated token requires special care. In a standard Transformer architecture, the prefill pass processes the entire prompt and produces the initial logit distribution from which the first output token is sampled. From the client’s perspective, the elapsed time between dispatching the request and receiving that initial token constitutes the time to first token (TTFT), whereas the average delay between subsequent tokens defines the time per output token (TPOT). Production serving systems treat TTFT and TPOT as separate service-level objectives because prefill and decode compete directly for shared accelerator compute and memory bandwidth. Indeed, disaggregated serving architectures separate prefill and decode onto physically distinct accelerator pools to isolate these workloads and meet each objective independently (Zhong et al. 2024).

Zhong, Yinmin, Shengyu Liu, Junda Chen, Jianbo Hu, Yibo Zhu, Xuanzhe Liu, Xin Jin, and Hao Zhang. 2024. “DistServe: Disaggregating Prefill and Decoding for Goodput-Optimized Large Language Model Serving.” 18th USENIX Symposium on Operating Systems Design and Implementation (OSDI 24), July, 193–210.

When telemetry includes the sampling and transmission of the first token in TTFT, the remaining decode phase accounts for only the subsequent \(\OutputLength-1\) tokens in a completion of total length \(\OutputLength\). For a streamed response, adding client-measured TTFT, the inter-token arrival intervals across the remaining \(\OutputLength-1\) tokens, and trailing response deserialization captures total call latency without charging the first token twice. Client-observed TTFT differs substantially from server-side prefill execution time because it also absorbs network transit and queueing delay. Furthermore, because network transports and serving runtimes frequently batch streamed tokens into multi-token network chunks, observed chunk-arrival intervals do not always reflect raw token-generation intervals on the accelerator.

Setting an output token limit caps generation volume rather than execution speed; an overloaded inference queue can easily consume an agent’s entire timeout budget before prefill even completes. Meaningful latency benchmarks must therefore explicitly record the model architecture, prompt and completion lengths, prefix-cache conditions, and cluster load. Even when an agent executes single-threaded, sequential turns, the underlying serving system rarely processes that agent in isolation. Modern inference engines employ continuous batching to schedule, admit, and retire individual requests at the granularity of single token-generation steps (Yu et al. 2022), interleaving the agent’s decode steps with requests from dozens of other concurrent clients.

2.8.2 Per-call work estimate

A baseline analytical model of per-call execution gives input processing and output generation separate per-token time floors, because different physical constraints govern the two phases. A dense Transformer with \(\Parameters\) parameters executes approximately \(2\Parameters\) floating-point operations for each token it processes, in prefill and decode alike. What differs is how often the weights must be read. Prefill reads each weight tensor from accelerator memory once per call and reuses it across all \(\SequenceLength\) prompt positions, so a long prompt spreads one weight read over thousands of tokens of arithmetic, and each prompt token costs about \(2\Parameters\) operations at the peak arithmetic rate \(\PeakRate\). Single-stream decode produces one token per step, so it reads every weight once per generated token. Each output token therefore moves the full weight payload of \(\Parameters\,\ElementStorageSize\) bytes (with \(\ElementStorageSize\) bytes per parameter) across a memory interface of bandwidth \(\Bandwidth\), and its arithmetic finishes long before those bytes arrive.

These two regimes are the two limbs of the roofline model (Williams et al. 2009), which bounds attainable throughput by the lesser of the peak arithmetic rate and the memory bandwidth multiplied by the work done per byte moved. Prefill does much work per byte and sits under the arithmetic limb; low-batch decode does little and sits under the bandwidth limb, a split that empirical evaluations of Transformer inference confirm (Pope et al. 2023). The two floors are

Williams, Samuel, Andrew Waterman, and David Patterson. 2009. “Roofline: An Insightful Visual Performance Model for Multicore Architectures.” Communications of the ACM 52 (4): 65–76. https://doi.org/10.1145/1498765.1498785.

\[\PerTokenPrefillTime \approx \frac{2\Parameters}{\PeakRate}, \qquad \PerTokenDecodeTime \ge \frac{\Parameters\,\ElementStorageSize}{\Bandwidth} \tag{7}\]

The lowercase symbols are per-token floors. Under this model the phase durations of equation 6 are \(\PrefillTime \approx \SequenceLength\,\PerTokenPrefillTime\) and \(\DecodeInterval \approx \OutputLength\,\PerTokenDecodeTime\). Dividing the two floors cancels the parameter count \(\Parameters\):

\[\frac{\PerTokenDecodeTime}{\PerTokenPrefillTime} \approx \frac{\PeakRate \, \ElementStorageSize}{2 \, \Bandwidth} \tag{8}\]

Consider a concrete reference deployment: a dense model with 70.6B parameters stored in 8-bit precision (\(\ElementStorageSize = 1\) byte) on a single accelerator with an 8-bit peak rate of 1,979 TFLOP/s and a memory bandwidth of 3.35 TB/s. Each token costs 141.2 GFLOP of arithmetic, and dividing by the peak rate gives a prefill floor of 71.35 μs per prompt token. Each decode step reads 70.6 GB of weights, and dividing by the bandwidth gives a decode floor of 21.075 ms per generated token. Under equation 8, decoding a single output token therefore takes the same hardware time as ingesting roughly 295 prompt tokens. This asymmetry is the content of output-bound latency (principle 2), under which each plain, unbatched decode step incurs a full weight read in this model, so output volume can dominate call latency. The ratio reflects weight-dominated, unbatched decode against peak-throughput prefill, not a measured cloud service rate or a commercial billing tier.

↪ Both execution floors and their operating boundaries are developed in Cost Asymmetry Between Input Prefill and Output Generation.

This analytical budget charges each prompt token once at the prefill floor and reserves one decode floor step for each generated token. It is an idealized hardware baseline rather than a substitute for wall-clock measurement: it omits network transit, queueing delay, attention quadratics, and runtime software overhead. It also assumes standard autoregressive generation without speculative decoding. In production, a serving system that batches concurrent client requests, distributes model weights across tensor-parallel accelerators (sharding individual weight matrices across multiple devices), or reuses cached attention state substantially alters the relationship between token count and elapsed time. The analytical estimate is therefore ideal for comparing interface formats and output volumes, but certifying a production deadline requires measuring latency directly under realistic serving load.

Applying this baseline to the first repair turn demonstrates the structural dominance of output tokens. A prompt of 8,000 tokens contributes 0.571 s of prefill work, while generating a reply of 200 tokens contributes 4.215 s of decode execution. The total call estimate is 4.786 s. Although output constitutes only 2.4 percent of the call’s total tokens, it accounts for 88.1 percent of the required processing time. Pruning redundant output tokens therefore reduces overall latency far more effectively than removing an identical number of prompt tokens. At the same time, an agent benefits from a cheaper invocation only if the model still receives sufficient diagnostic context to generate a correct candidate; engineers must prune verbosity from the output format, not critical evidence from the prompt.

2.8.3 Multi-turn trajectory cost accumulation

Even when individual turns produce concise outputs, a multi-turn trajectory can still generate an immense prompt ingestion workload. Consider an agent runtime that maintains an append-only execution log and re-transmits the full cumulative history on every invocation. If the initial turn contains \(\BasePromptLength\) prompt tokens and each subsequent turn appends \(\TurnAppendLength\) tokens of combined model output and tool observations, a trajectory spanning a horizon of \(\TurnHorizon\) turns submits:

\[\PromptTokensSent = \sum_{j=0}^{\TurnHorizon-1} \left(\BasePromptLength + \PriorTurnCount\,\TurnAppendLength\right) = \TurnHorizon \BasePromptLength + \TurnAppendLength \frac{\TurnHorizon(\TurnHorizon-1)}{2} \tag{9}\]

prompt tokens in total. The quadratic growth in equation 9 arises directly from the naive strategy of repeatedly re-sending accumulated history, rather than from an intrinsic requirement of agentic reasoning. Implementing sliding-window context buffers, trimming redundant tool payloads, or summarizing historical turns transforms this workload. Furthermore, a stateful serving interface can maintain conversation state on the server, avoiding redundant network re-transmission even when the model’s active attention window spans the full trajectory.

Re-sent input grows with the square of the horizon; prefix reuse keeps fresh prefill linear.

Fortunately, repeated prompt input does not necessitate repeated matrix computation. Prefix caching enables the inference engine to retain and reuse previously computed Key-Value (KV) activations for prompt prefixes that remain identical across consecutive requests (Zheng et al. 2024). Successful KV reuse requires an exact match across token IDs, model weights, and positional encodings, as well as sufficient accelerator memory to retain the cache blocks. Modifying an early system prompt instruction or altering a tool schema invalidates all downstream cached activations from that modification point onward. Even identical source text fails to hit the cache if tokenizer configurations, chat templates, or special tokens diverge.

To quantify the benefits of prefix reuse, assume an inference service that caches all preceding prompt prefixes but runs fresh prefill over each newly appended turn suffix. This newly appended suffix contains the model’s previous response and the subsequent tool output. Across all \(\TurnHorizon\) turns, the cumulative number of prompt tokens requiring fresh prefill drops to \(\BasePromptLength + (\TurnHorizon-1)\,\TurnAppendLength\). This baseline conservatively assumes the runtime does not preserve the KV activations generated during decoding; advanced engines can retain those generated activations as well, avoiding fresh prefill over earlier model outputs. Holding these assumptions constant isolates the exact architectural benefit of prefix caching.

Napkin Math 2.2: The price of an append-only trajectory
Problem: A coding agent runs 30 turns. Its first prompt has 8,000 tokens, each turn appends 1,500 tokens, and each call emits 200 output tokens. Assume that every prompt fits the service’s context limit and that compatible earlier prompt state remains available for reuse. How much input does the trajectory supply, and how does prompt-prefix reuse change its estimated processing work?

Math: Applying the arithmetic series in equation 9 gives \(30\times 8,000+1,500\times\frac{30\times 29}{2}=892,500\) prompt tokens. Total output is \(30\times 200=6,000\) tokens. Without reuse, input therefore accounts for 99.3 percent of the combined input and output token count, although it does not account for that share of processing time.

With prompt-prefix reuse, fresh prefill covers \(8,000+29\times 1,500=51,500\) tokens. Dividing the original input count by this fresh-prefill count gives a 17.3× reduction. At the reference prefill floor, the prefill-work estimate falls from 63.68 s to 3.67 s. Decode work stays at 126.45 s; caching earlier input does not remove the task’s new responses.

Systems insight: Append-only context makes input work grow with the square of the horizon. Doubling the trajectory to 60 turns multiplies re-sent input by 3.5× but fresh prefill under prefix reuse by only 1.9×, because reuse converts quadratic growth into a linear trajectory. Prefix reuse therefore serves as the primary lever for minimizing the input component of \(\AttemptCost\) in the cost per accepted task (equation 1.2), with its value compounding as horizons lengthen. While caching preserves the full decision context without loss of information, cache eviction and cross-worker request routing can dilute these theoretical savings in production.

In the illustrated repair turn, output length dominates the single-call estimate. For the append-only workload under high batching described here, uncached prefill can dominate aggregate serving work. Prefix reuse acts directly on the input component; its effect on end-to-end latency depends on how much that component contributes. Wall-clock latency governs the end-to-end responsiveness experienced by a single agent trajectory. Because each sequential turn depends strictly on the outcome of prior tool executions, an agent cannot parallelize its own dependent turns; every output token remains a sequential step on the critical execution path, even when the server concurrently batches that step with other requests. Computational work, by contrast, dictates cluster throughput, hardware provisioning, and aggregate operational expenditure. When a serving cluster batches dozens of concurrent streams, the memory weight reads are amortized across requests. Under high batching, the amortized service time per decoded token drops from the memory-bandwidth floor toward the compute-bound arithmetic limit \(2\Parameters/\PeakRate\) of equation 7, the same efficiency as prompt prefill, as the batched-step bound derived in Cost Asymmetry Between Input Prefill and Output Generation shows. In this regime, total cluster workload scales directly with aggregate token volume. For an append-only agent, that token volume is overwhelmingly dominated by re-sent prompt history, so uncached prefill dominates the aggregate work estimate for this workload when the cluster maintains high batching and has memory for the attention state.

Which term an engineer should attack therefore depends on who waits for the output, and the repair agent alone cannot show the contrast. Its consumer is a compilation and test pipeline that must wait for a syntactically complete response. The support agent (lighthouse 1.4) instead streams output tokens to a person reading as they arrive. The consumer requirements determine which latency terms these two illustrated workloads prioritize.

Lighthouse 2.1: Call latency for two lighthouses
Support agent: A customer-operations turn submits an 8,192-token prompt (consisting of policy documentation, customer account history, and recent dialogue) and streams an 150-token response to a human user in a live chat interface. At the chapter’s reference hardware floors, prefill takes 0.58 s and generating the full reply requires 3.2 s. However, token streaming displays the initial words immediately following queueing and prefill; the remaining tokens arrive at approximately 47 tokens per second, which easily outpaces human reading speed. Under these lengths and streaming rates, TTFT governs how soon the user can begin reading. When 6,144 prompt tokens represent a common prefix of system instructions, organizational policies, and tool definitions shared across all concurrent sessions, prefix caching leaves only 2,048 tokens of fresh prefill, which takes just 0.15 s. In a loaded cluster, admission queueing rapidly becomes the dominant contributor to first-token latency.

Repair agent: The initial repair turn commits 88.1 percent of its estimated hardware work to generating a 200-token response. The agent runtime can parse, validate, and execute the generated patch only after receiving the entire payload. Decode duration dominates this repair estimate, making the compactness of the requested code representation its primary optimization lever.

Systems insight: The illustrated support turn prioritizes TTFT because the user can read while the remaining tokens arrive, whereas output volume dominates the illustrated repair call, whose consumer waits for a complete patch. Shortening the support reply does not directly remove queueing or prefill from first-token latency. Caching the repair prompt reduces its input work and input cost, but that saving contributes little to this output-dominated estimate. Different prompt lengths, streaming rates, or serving load can change the binding term; measured phase durations and queueing determine which intervention improves the latency the consumer waits on.

Retaining intermediate activations in memory introduces an explicit storage cost. Caching a long prefix occupies accelerator high-bandwidth memory that cannot be allocated to concurrent request batches. Holding that KV state active across long external tool invocations pins memory that no generation is using. When memory pressure triggers cache eviction, the serving system discards intermediate attention activations rather than the underlying context; the agent runtime can always reconstruct the prompt from its local logs, albeit at the cost of recomputing the prefill forward pass. Context selection and KV-cache management thus represent two distinct system decisions: context engineering determines what evidence the model observes, whereas cache retention policies govern whether storing derived attention state is worth the memory footprint.

↪ The choice to keep, move, or drop attention state held through a tool wait is developed in Paused-State Policies.

Wall-clock latency, hardware compute work, and billed API cost represent distinct accounting dimensions. Commercial inference providers typically bill uncached prompt tokens, cached prompt tokens, and generated completion tokens at widely divergent rates, whereas on-premise infrastructure amortizes fixed hardware acquisition and power costs across dynamic utilization. Consequently, token-count ratios do not directly mirror monetary expense. At the full task horizon, end-to-end expenditure also encompasses failed tool executions, retry attempts, sandboxed validation runs, and idle accelerator reservations during tool waits. The analytical per-call estimate provides only one fundamental building block of this comprehensive systems budget.

Checkpoint 2.3: Pricing calls and trajectories

A call’s token count and its elapsed execution time quantify distinct facets of the overall system budget.

Deconstructing the model invocation budget exposes two primary sources of computational waste: generating superfluous tokens that the downstream interface does not require, and recomputing prompt representations that the serving system could readily cache. Optimizing these efficiency levers does not guarantee task success, however; a compact diff that fails to compile or a cached prompt that leads to an incorrect edit wastes the entire invocation. Selecting an optimal agent interface demands evaluating candidate patch quality, downstream failure modes, and end-to-end resource consumption under identical operational benchmarks.

2.9 Interface Benchmarking

When an automated repair agent modifies connection-pool locking logic, its output payload does not need to repeat the entire source file. The model could emit a fully rewritten file, or it could target a specific code block and emit only the replacement text. Emitting a localized edit consumes far fewer generation tokens by omitting unchanged code, but it creates an operational dependency: the execution runtime must locate the exact target coordinates in the target file. Choosing an interface is therefore not a superficial formatting choice; it directly determines both the computational cost of generating a candidate and the operational preconditions required for the runtime to apply it.

High schema parse rates alone cannot validate an interface design. A whole-file replacement might parse into valid JSON yet arrive long after the service deadline because the model spent tens of seconds generating unchanged lines. Conversely, a compact diff might arrive in about 2.3 s yet fail to match its target anchors against current repository state. Neither outcome delivers an accepted repair. A rigorous empirical comparison must track the model call’s completion status, account for all compute resources consumed across retries, and validate the resulting artifact against uniform acceptance criteria.

2.9.1 Interface design dimensions

Representation granularity dictates how much context the model must emit to describe a mutation. At one extreme, a whole-file representation returns the entire file content, re-emitting hundreds of lines of untouched code alongside the modification. At the other extreme, a localized edit supplies only a target locator and the delta relative to the baseline file. Consider an anchored search-and-replace edit: it specifies an exact snippet of source text to find and the replacement text to substitute. If that anchor string is missing from the file or matches multiple locations, the runtime cannot infer a unique mutation. Rewriting the entire file eliminates anchor-matching ambiguities, but it introduces a different validation burden: the runtime must ensure the model did not silently overwrite unrelated functions or drop concurrent modifications made by other processes.

Serialization represents a distinct and orthogonal design dimension. Either granularity can use JSON: one object might store a target path and complete source buffer, while another stores a path, a search block, and a replacement snippet. Custom delimiters, such as Markdown code fences or unified diff headers, can frame either payload just as easily. The actual token overhead depends on tokenizer vocabulary and structural markers, not on common folklore that JSON is inherently bloated while patch syntax is always compact. Emitting thousands of tokens for an unmodified file is a penalty imposed by coarse representation granularity, not by the serialization format wrapping it.

The constraint mechanism introduces a third independent axis. An agent architecture can elicit structured outputs through fine-tuning, in-context demonstrations, or runtime grammar masking during token decoding. These mechanisms can also operate together; for example, fine-tuned models frequently run under grammar masks to prevent syntax drift. The formal language guarantees established in section 2.7 ensure that generated tokens conform to a specified grammar, but they guarantee grammatical form, not execution semantics. A grammar mask cannot guarantee that an anchor snippet exists within the repository, nor that the proposed patch repairs the underlying race condition. If an evaluation alters both serialization framing and constraint methods simultaneously, it benchmarks bundled interface variants rather than isolating the causal impact of any single architectural choice.

2.9.2 Matched-budget comparison

To evaluate competing interfaces fairly, an experimental harness must enforce matched budgets across all trials. In the connection-pool repair benchmark, every trial starts from an identical repository snapshot and defect description, and the harness holds constant the model weights, sampling parameters, diagnostic tooling, and acceptance test suites. Each interface also operates under identical resource ceilings (such as maximum wall-clock time and token allocations) and the same retry policy. Instruction prompts and response payloads will naturally exhibit different token counts, because prompt overhead and verbosity are intrinsic properties of the interface under test. Matched budgets do not require equal token consumption across designs; rather, they subject each candidate to identical operating boundaries and accounting rules.

↪ Matched-budget comparison of complete agent policies, charging every initiated trial to the cost per accepted task, is developed in Matched-budget comparison.

The accounting boundary must be defined explicitly. An invocation deadline measures single-turn inference latency, concluding when the runtime receives and normalizes the model’s generated response. A task deadline governs the complete agent lifecycle, including payload parsing, workspace patching, test compilation, and test execution. Every retry, unparsable output, and failing test execution draws against this cumulative task budget. Benchmarks that grant free retries following malformed responses subsidize brittle interfaces and corrupt comparative latency metrics.

To pinpoint system failures without masking catastrophic errors, the benchmark harness must decouple intermediate execution stages from final task acceptance (table 5). The total number of initiated trials must remain the denominator for all top-level success metrics. Discarding unparsable responses or timed-out calls artificially inflates reported accuracy. Conditional rates, such as the proportion of cleanly applied edits that pass functional tests, serve as valuable diagnostic telemetry, but engineers must always state the conditioning filter explicitly.

Production serving conditions must also be recorded alongside payload statistics. Shared inference infrastructure introduces substantial latency variance: KV-cache hits, dynamic batching contention, and host-level queueing delays alter observed wall-clock execution even when prompt and completion lengths remain constant. A few favorable measurements during quiet periods do not prove deadline reliability. In shared production clusters, tail latency governs service-level objective violations, because the slowest requests dictate pipeline throughput (Dean and Barroso 2013). The immediate engineering question is whether an interface representation leaves sufficient headroom under the tail-latency envelope to generate and validate candidate fixes.

Dean, Jeffrey, and Luiz André Barroso. 2013. “The Tail at Scale.” Communications of the ACM 56 (2): 74–80.

2.9.3 A deadline-constrained representation choice

Applying the per-token cost model highlights the severe latency penalty of emitting unchanged text, tracing performance bottlenecks directly to interface granularity rather than serialization envelopes.

Generating unchanged code, not reading the prompt, pushes the whole file past the deadline.

Napkin Math 2.3: A repair representation under an invocation deadline
Problem: A repair invocation begins with 8,000 baseline context tokens and operates under an invocation deadline of 15 s. Emitting a whole-file replacement requires 350 additional instruction tokens and 1,850 output tokens. Emitting a localized edit requires 120 additional instruction tokens and 80 output tokens. These token counts represent scenario parameters. Using the prefill and autoregressive decoding rates established earlier, which interface representation fits within the simplified service-work budget?

Math: The service-work model charges prompt prefill at 71.35 μs per token and autoregressive decoding at 21.075 ms per token. To isolate inference execution, this baseline calculation omits request serialization, network transit, server-side queueing, and subsequent validation runs. Multiplying each interface’s token profile by these unit costs yields:

\((8,000+350)\times 0.00007135\,\text{s}+1,850\times 0.021075\,\text{s}\approx 39.58\,\text{s}\)

\((8,000+120)\times 0.00007135\,\text{s}+80\times 0.021075\,\text{s}\approx 2.27\,\text{s}\)

The whole-file estimate exceeds the deadline by a wide margin, whereas the localized edit finishes well within the allocated budget. The localized representation provides a 17.5× reduction in estimated service work, with token decoding accounting for 98.5 percent of the whole-file latency. In production, normalized invocation outcomes and observed wall-clock times determine whether the candidate arrived before deadline expiration.

To evaluate downstream yield, consider a second hypothetical scenario where localized edits complete on time with valid syntax, but only 95 percent resolve to an applicable edit on the codebase. Among those applicable edits, assume 60 percent pass the required test suite. The fraction of all initiated trials that yield an accepted repair is:

\(0.95\times 0.60=0.57\)

This 57 percent yield is the product of the applicability rate and the conditional test-pass rate, requiring no independence assumption between stages. Invocations that miss deadlines or emit invalid syntax reduce this end-to-end yield even further.

Systems insight: When output generation threatens an invocation deadline, shrinking representation granularity is the primary engineering lever. Because autoregressive decoding is memory-bandwidth bound, each generated token costs roughly 295 prompt prefill tokens at hardware reference floors (principle 2). Whole-file generation squanders almost all of its 39.6 s runtime emitting unchanged code, while the localized edit meets the deadline with a 6.6× safety margin. However, compact representations trade decoding latency for applicability risk: localized edits require exact anchor matching, which restricts the passing yield to 57 percent in this scenario. That hypothetical yield forms the acceptance probability \(\AcceptanceProbability\) that governs the amortized cost per accepted task (equation 1.2).

The invocation deadline functions as an absolute admission filter. Under these timing parameters, a whole-file response cannot even enter evaluation, rendering its potential code quality irrelevant. If system designers relax the deadline beyond the whole-file threshold, the trade-off shifts: applicability and patch correctness become decisive. Under generous latency budgets, a localized representation that frequently targets stale anchors or ambiguous line offsets quickly loses its advantage over a whole-file approach that reliably delivers an integrated solution.

Checkpoint 2.4: Reconsidering the interface when conditions change

The repair interface must be reassessed whenever the workspace state or resource budget shifts.

Rigorous interface benchmarking unifies model call contracts with systems economics. The request specifies the context and output allowances; the normalized response confirms whether the model delivered a completed payload before the deadline; and tiered evaluation metrics determine whether the proposed mutation reliably solves the task within resource constraints. Assuming that a valid schema implies an applicable patch, or that a fast diff implies defect-free code, collapses these stages into one, and that confusion remains among the most common failure modes in agent systems design.

2.10 Fallacies and Pitfalls

A code repair request can return promptly yet name the wrong file, or return the right patch long after its latency budget has expired. In autonomous execution loops, engineers frequently conflate distinct operational boundaries, confusing inference execution with total round-trip latency, syntactic validity with semantic correctness, and transport success with task completion. These failures expose several persistent mistaken assumptions about the model call.

Fallacy: One model response has a fixed cost.

Output length governs the bulk of execution time in the illustrated unbatched interface comparison. At its reference hardware floors, sequentially decoding a token takes far more time than processing a prompt token in parallel. In the interface comparison, regenerating a whole-file representation requires 39.6 s, whereas emitting a localized patch for the identical bug requires only 2.3 s, a 17.5× speedup that arises almost entirely during token generation (section 2.8). Engineers must therefore budget input and output tokens as separate resource dimensions rather than treating an invocation as an atomic unit of cost. Furthermore, wall-clock budgets must span the entire invocation boundary, including admission queueing and network transport; setting a maximum token limit bounds generation volume, but it does not control the time at which a response finally arrives.

Pitfall: Treating a completed invocation as a completed task.

A status of COMPLETED merely indicates that the serving engine finished generating tokens within the parameters of its invocation contract. It does not mean the produced output is correct or even usable. In the hypothetical interface scenario, every localized call completed on time, yet only 57 percent of those trials produced patches that passed the automated repair checks. Similarly, an agent may emit a syntactically parseable file path right before being cut off by a context limit, illustrating why downstream execution must verify generation status before triggering actions. Robust systems explicitly record two distinct facts for every invocation: whether a complete candidate artifact arrived from the generator, and whether independent verification evidence confirms that the artifact satisfies the task’s acceptance standard.

Fallacy: Valid JSON means a safe and correct tool call.

A JSON object satisfies its schema as long as its fields match the declared types, regardless of whether the payload is safe, authorized, or logically sound. A tool call containing a file path string conforms to schema validation whether that path names the intended target, an outdated file, or an unauthorized directory outside the permitted workspace. As table 3 lays out, schema checks establish structural validity, whereas authorization policies and task evidence govern security boundaries and functional correctness. The same gap recurs at the behavioral level: a test checker that exhibits a false accept rate of 0.65 on defective patches still leaves 14.37 percent of its passing candidates defective under these assumed rates. Furthermore, under grammar-constrained decoding, an interrupted sequence remains an incomplete, broken payload even if every token emitted prior to truncation was structurally legal.

Pitfall: Collapsing every unsuccessful outcome into the same retry.

Blindly retrying a failed call repeats the underlying root cause alongside the request. If a whole-file generation request misses a 15 s deadline, immediately resending the identical prompt spends another 39.6 s of estimated compute only to time out once again. Similarly, a response that exceeds its output budget truncates at the exact same token allowance, and a request that triggers a safety refusal elicits the same refusal on retry. Rather than treating all failures as transient network errors, an agent runtime must normalize execution status codes and dispatch targeted recovery policies: a bounded exponential backoff for transient service outages, a larger token allowance or more compact prompt representation for truncation, and a fresh environment observation when an edit fails to match its target.

Fallacy: Greedy decoding makes a served call reproducible.

Greedy selection fixes the token choice for a given logit vector, but the serving infrastructure computes that vector, and two distinct causes can change it. The first operates even on a healthy serving path, where batch-dependent floating-point reduction order shifts closely contested logits in their least significant bits, enough to flip an argmax and redirect every later token. The second is a change to the serving path itself. In war story 2.1, misrouting and a miscompiled top-\(\TopTokenSampleCount\) operation changed model outputs for identical requests, affecting 16 percent of one model’s requests at peak impact, so an identical prompt succeeded on one attempt and failed on the next. Pinning the temperature addresses neither cause. Systems engineers should record the model revision and serving configuration with every invocation, and replay recorded responses whenever the goal is to reproduce downstream runtime behavior deterministically.

Each of these corrections preserves an essential architectural boundary. Resource consumption measures computational expenditure, completion status tracks generator termination, structural checks validate payload syntax, and acceptance testing confirms task success. Conflating these dimensions invites fragile error recovery and cascading failures. Keeping these boundaries distinct allows higher-level orchestration loops to compose individual model invocations as predictable system components without inheriting unstated assumptions.

2.11 Summary

A repair agent interacts with a foundation model through a strictly bounded request-response interface. The request bundles messages, tool definitions, generation limits, and sampling parameters into a single serialized payload, but this handoff never delegates the runtime’s authority to the model. The response contract preserves explicit boundaries between final-answer candidates, tool execution proposals, and incomplete or failed generations. The runtime retains exclusive ownership over authorizing side effects and gathering the evidence required to accept a result. This division of responsibility is invariant closure (principle 1) applied to a single call, the case every later chapter extends.

Table 6 summarizes the quantitative relations governing model invocations, tracking admission constraints, generation dynamics, verification limits, and trajectory-level resource scaling.

Key Takeaways: A model call is a bounded proposal interface
  • Budget the representation the service receives: Count serialized requests using the target model’s tokenizer and reserve explicit headroom for generated tokens. Context window limits, provider message caps, and network timeouts restrict distinct dimensions of the invocation; raw character counts never reliably predict actual token consumption or admission success.
  • Completion admits a candidate, not an action: Inspect the response kind and normalized status before consuming the payload. While an incomplete generation provides useful diagnostic data for debugging, syntactic parseability alone never promotes a partial string into a valid candidate, nor does it grant permission to execute tool actions.
  • Structural guarantees have a stated scope: Grammar constraints enforce syntactic validity under their schema assumptions, whereas permission checks govern permissible side effects and verifiers supply empirical acceptance evidence. A passing test supports only the properties exercised; model self-review remains a candidate assessment rather than independent confirmation.
  • Output and repeated input incur different costs: One output token costs about 295 prompt tokens at the reference hardware floors. Generation length dominates the illustrated unbatched repair estimate; prompt length and queueing can bind under other conditions. An append-only conversational history causes re-sent input tokens to scale quadratically with the trajectory horizon. Compatible prefix reuse turns that quadratic growth into linear work, but cached key-value states must never serve as the authoritative record of task state.
  • Compare accepted results within the same budget: Full-file replacements and localized patch edits trade generation volume against patch applicability constraints. Evaluate these alternatives by measuring end-to-end task completion, total token expenditure, and identical acceptance checks, rather than treating serialization format or schema parse rates as an engineering verdict.

2.12 Historical Perspective and Further Reading

The foundation model call joins two intellectual lineages that developed independently for decades. The first is statistical language modeling. Shannon (1948) framed natural language as a discrete stochastic process, treating English as a sequence of symbols drawn from conditional probability distributions and generating text from successively longer context windows. Bengio et al. (2003) replaced high-dimensional count tables with a continuous neural function over distributed word representations, establishing the autoregressive next-token factorization that modern architectures still use. The Transformer made that formulation parallelizable across token positions during training (Vaswani et al. 2017). In production serving, that same architectural choice creates a fundamental operational split: an initial prompt prefill phase often limited by arithmetic throughput, followed by a small-batch autoregressive generation loop often limited by memory bandwidth (Pope et al. 2023). Subword tokenization algorithms borrowed from data compression solved the out-of-vocabulary dilemma (Gage 1994; Sennrich et al. 2016), and scaling laws subsequently produced models capable of task execution directly from natural language prompts (Brown et al. 2020). Bommasani et al. (2021) formalized the concept of foundation models while warning of their central systems risk: when hundreds of applications build on a single upstream base model, any flaw, bias, or failure mode in that model propagates throughout the entire software stack.

Shannon, Claude E. 1948. “A Mathematical Theory of Communication.” Bell System Technical Journal 27 (3): 379–423. https://doi.org/10.1002/j.1538-7305.1948.tb01338.x.
Bengio, Yoshua, Réjean Ducharme, Pascal Vincent, and Christian Jauvin. 2003. “A Neural Probabilistic Language Model.” Journal of Machine Learning Research 3: 1137–55.
Vaswani, Ashish, Noam Shazeer, Niki Parmar, Jakob Uszkoreit, Llion Jones, Aidan N. Gomez, Lukasz Kaiser, and Illia Polosukhin. 2017. “Attention Is All You Need.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 30: 5998–6008.
Brown, Tom B., Benjamin Mann, Nick Ryder, Melanie Subbiah, Jared Kaplan, Prafulla Dhariwal, Arvind Neelakantan, et al. 2020. “Language Models Are Few-Shot Learners.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems 33: 1877–901. https://doi.org/10.48550/arxiv.2005.14165.
Fan, Angela, Mike Lewis, and Yann Dauphin. 2018. “Hierarchical Neural Story Generation.” Proceedings of the 56th Annual Meeting of the Association for Computational Linguistics (Volume 1: Long Papers), 889–98. https://doi.org/10.18653/v1/P18-1082.
Yu, Gyeong-In, Joo Seong Jeong, Geon-Woo Kim, Soojeong Kim, and Byung-Gon Chun. 2022. “Orca: A Distributed Serving System for Transformer-Based Generative Models.” 16th USENIX Symposium on Operating Systems Design and Implementation (OSDI 22), 521–38.
Zheng, Lianmin, Liangsheng Ying, Zhenghao Yin, Cody Hao Liu, Eric Yu, Prakhar Patel, Zhipeng Yang, Anthony K. Joseph, and Ion Stoica. 2024. “SGLang: Efficient Execution of Structured Language Model Programs.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2312.07104, ahead of print. https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2312.07104.

Decoding strategies evolved alongside model scale to mitigate systematic failure modes in generation. Early inference relied on greedy search or beam search to maximize likelihood, but these objectives frequently trapped models in bland, repetitive loops. Truncated sampling addressed this degeneration by restricting candidates to the head of the output distribution, first through a fixed token count in top-\(\TopTokenSampleCount\) sampling (Fan et al. 2018) and subsequently through top-\(\NucleusProbabilityMass\) (nucleus) sampling, which adapts the truncation boundary dynamically to the distribution’s cumulative probability mass (Holtzman et al. 2020). Speculative decoding decoupled generation latency from model parameter scale by using a lightweight draft model to generate token candidates that the larger target model verifies in parallel (Leviathan et al. 2023; Chen et al. 2023). At the infrastructure level, serving engines moved beyond rigid static batching: Orca introduced iteration-level scheduling to interleave prefill and decode phases dynamically (Yu et al. 2022), while SGLang demonstrated how prefix caching across requests amortizes repetitive prompt computations (Zheng et al. 2024). Together, these runtime innovations transformed the per-token computational costs analyzed in this chapter into shared, schedulable execution pipelines.

The second lineage stems from distributed systems engineering and access mediation across untrusted boundaries. When Birrell and Nelson introduced remote procedure calls, their goal was to make remote execution feel like a local function call, yet they proved that network boundaries inevitably expose failure semantics that local calls never experience, most notably states in which the outcome of an operation remains fundamentally undecidable (Birrell and Nelson 1984). Decades of systems architecture reinforce that lower-level abstractions cannot reliably guarantee end-to-end semantics; as the end-to-end argument demonstrates, true correctness must ultimately be validated by the participating endpoints (Saltzer et al. 1984). In an agent runtime, an external service returning a COMPLETED status serves as an execution hint rather than a definitive guarantee of success. Similarly, classical security engineering established the principle of complete mediation (Saltzer and Schroeder 1975), which requires validating every access request against current authority rather than trusting past authorization, which is the discipline an agent runtime must enforce when dispatching model-proposed tool calls. Finally, parsing theory provides the formal prefix property (Knuth 1965) that underpins modern constrained decoding, ensuring token-by-token alignment with structured schema grammars (Scholak et al. 2021; Willard and Louf 2023). While sampling payloads dynamically across an open vocabulary is a modern capability, the defensive engineering required to safely govern those calls is built entirely on these proven systems foundations.

Scholak, Torsten, Nathan Schucher, and Dzmitry Bahdanau. 2021. “PICARD: Parsing Incrementally for Constrained Auto-Regressive Decoding from Language Models.” Proceedings of the 2021 Conference on Empirical Methods in Natural Language Processing, 9895–901. https://doi.org/10.18653/v1/2021.emnlp-main.779.

The following six papers provide essential reading on the conceptual and systems foundations of foundation model runtimes:

  • Birrell and Nelson (1984) introduces the mechanics of remote procedure calls and catalogs their failure semantics. It remains the classic reference for why invocation contracts across process boundaries must handle network partitions, cancellations, and undecidable execution states.
  • Saltzer et al. (1984) presents the end-to-end argument in system design, articulating why lower architectural layers cannot reliably guarantee higher-level application correctness. It underpins why an agent runtime cannot treat external service status codes (such as COMPLETED) as substitutes for independent outcome validation.
  • Holtzman et al. (2020) analyzes the degeneration of likelihood-maximizing decoding in open-ended text generation and introduces nucleus (top-\(\NucleusProbabilityMass\)) sampling. It demonstrates that decoding algorithms represent deliberate engineering trade-offs that govern output entropy, coherence, and candidate diversity.
  • Pope et al. (2023) develops an analytical roofline model of Transformer serving that separates compute-bound prompt prefill from memory-bandwidth-bound autoregressive decoding. It establishes the quantitative foundation for per-token latency floors, KV cache memory footprints, and batching trade-offs.
  • Willard and Louf (2023) introduces efficient constrained decoding by compiling regular expressions and context-free grammars into finite-state machines indexed over the model’s token vocabulary. It demonstrates how to enforce strict structural schemas at runtime with minimal per-token computational overhead.
  • Bommasani et al. (2021) surveys the technical capabilities and architectural risks of foundation models. Its analysis of model homogenization clarifies why centralized upstream dependencies require strict isolation boundaries, independent validation, and runtime guardrails across downstream applications.
Saltzer, Jerome H., David P. Reed, and David D. Clark. 1984. “End-to-End Arguments in System Design.” ACM Transactions on Computer Systems 2 (4): 277–88. https://doi.org/10.1145/357401.357402.
Holtzman, Ari, Jan Buys, Li Du, Maxwell Forbes, and Yejin Choi. 2020. “The Curious Case of Neural Text Degeneration.” Proceedings of the 8th International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR 2020).
Pope, Reiner, Sholto Douglas, Aakanksha Chowdhery, Jacob Devlin, James Bradbury, Jonathan Heek, Kefan Xiao, Shivani Agrawal, and Jeff Dean. 2023. “Efficiently Scaling Transformer Inference.” Proceedings of Machine Learning and Systems (MLSys) 5: 606–24.
Willard, Brandon T., and Rémi Louf. 2023. “Efficient Guided Generation for Large Language Models.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2307.09702.

2.13 Exercises

All quantities below are hypothetical problem givens, chosen to be round, and describe no particular model, accelerator, or service. Where a problem uses the per-token work floors of equation 7, take them as floors in the chapter’s sense, which exclude queueing, transfer, attention-dependent work, and serving overhead.

1. Admitting a request with a long observation (Basic). A repair call runs against an interface with a combined context window \(\ContextWindow = 32{,}768\) tokens, and the runtime reserves \(\OutputLimit = 4{,}096\) tokens of output headroom. System instructions and tool definitions serialize to 3,200 tokens and the retained conversation to 18,000. The newest tool observation, a test log, was estimated at 9,500 tokens by dividing its 38,000 characters by four.

  1. Compute \(\SequenceLength\), the input ceiling, and whether the request is admitted. If not, find the largest observation that fits with the reservation unchanged.
  2. A teammate proposes halving \(\OutputLimit\) instead of shortening the log. Does the request then fit? Past proposals in this workload have run to 3,000 output tokens; state what the smaller reservation risks and which status the runtime would then record.
  3. The model’s tokenizer reports the log as 11,400 tokens, because indentation and escaped quotation marks split into many short tokens. Recompute both options, and state which measurement the runtime must take before any admission decision.

2. Where a call’s time goes (Basic). A dense model with \(\Parameters = 40\) billion parameters stores 8-bit weights (\(\ElementStorageSize = 1\) byte) on an accelerator with peak rate \(\PeakRate = 1{,}000\) TFLOP/s and memory bandwidth \(\Bandwidth = 4\) TB/s.

  1. Compute \(\PerTokenPrefillTime\), \(\PerTokenDecodeTime\), and their ratio.
  2. The first repair turn sends a 12,000-token prompt and generates a 300-token reply. Compute the prefill and decode times at these floors, the share of estimated work spent generating, and the share of tokens that are output.
  3. Two optimizations are proposed: trimming 3,000 tokens of retrieved documentation from the prompt, or a terser proposal format that halves the reply. Compute the work each saves and choose one. Then say how 4-bit weights (\(\ElementStorageSize = 0.5\) byte) would change the floor ratio and whether they change your choice.

3. How much diversity a setting buys (Basic). At one decision point a model’s logits over four candidate tokens are \(\LogitVector_t = [8, 7, 5, 2]\), and a later selection stage wants a first token that differs from the top-scoring one so that it has a genuine alternative to compare.

  1. At \(\SamplingTemperature = 1\), compute the four probabilities and the set retained by top-\(\NucleusProbabilityMass\) sampling with \(\NucleusProbabilityMass = 0.90\), with its renormalized probabilities.
  2. Compute the probability that one draw differs from the top token under (i) top-\(\NucleusProbabilityMass\) at unit temperature and (ii) untruncated sampling at \(\SamplingTemperature = 0.5\). How many independent draws does each setting need so that at least one differs from the top token with probability at least 0.9?
  3. An engineer concludes that the lower temperature is the “more accurate” setting because the top token receives more mass. Explain what the calculation does and does not show, and name what should choose the setting.

4. Paying for an append-only history (Intermediate). A repair trajectory runs \(\TurnHorizon = 40\) turns with \(\BasePromptLength = 10{,}000\) tokens and appends \(\TurnAppendLength = 2{,}000\) tokens per turn; each call generates 250 tokens. Hypothetical prices per million tokens are $3 for uncached input, $0.30 for cached input, and $15 for output.

  1. Compute the total prompt tokens supplied, the fresh-prefill tokens under prompt-prefix reuse (section 2.8), and the total output tokens. Repeat the first two for \(\TurnHorizon = 80\) and report each growth factor.
  2. Compute the trajectory’s dollar cost with no reuse and with every reused prefix billed as cached input. What share of the dollars is input in each case?
  3. A developer adds the current timestamp to the first line of the system prompt so the model knows the time. Compute the new cost, explain the mechanism, and redesign the request so the model still receives the time.

5. Ordering fallible checks (Intermediate). Suppose 25 percent of generated connection-pool patches violate the required concurrency property. Checker A (compile and unit tests) passes defective patches with \(\VerifierFalseAcceptanceRate = 0.5\) and valid ones with probability 0.9, at $0.02 per run. Checker B (a concurrency stress test) has \(\VerifierFalseAcceptanceRate = 0.2\) and passes valid patches with probability 0.8, at $0.30 per run. The task contract tolerates at most 5 percent defective patches among those accepted.

  1. Compute \(\Pr(\text{Defective} \mid \text{PASS})\) for A alone and for B alone. Does either meet the contract?
  2. Assume the two checkers’ verdicts are conditionally independent given a patch’s validity. Compute the residual for requiring both, the fraction of candidates accepted, and the expected checking cost per candidate when A runs first and B runs only on A’s passes, and when B runs first.
  3. Measurement shows that among defective patches that pass A, B passes 40 percent rather than 20 percent, because both miss races that the unit tests never schedule. Assume that the checkers’ verdicts remain conditionally independent for valid patches. Recompute the residual, state whether the pipeline still meets the contract, and propose one change to the checking design that attacks the correlation rather than adding another run of either checker.

6. Reject early, or never emit (Intermediate). A tool-calling service handles 10,000 proposals a day with prompts of 8,000 tokens and successful replies of 400 tokens, at \(\PerTokenPrefillTime = 80\) µs and \(\PerTokenDecodeTime = 10\) ms per token. Of these proposals, 6 percent violate the argument schema; the violation first appears at decode step 40 on average, and such a response otherwise runs 600 tokens before stopping. Every failed attempt is followed by one retry, which you may assume succeeds. Successful retries and completed grammar-constrained responses each contain 400 tokens. Measure added latency relative to one successful unmasked call; ignore cancellation signaling, validation, queueing, and transfer delays, and allow the retry to start immediately after local rejection. Three designs are proposed: validate after completion; validate the stream and cancel at the first illegal token (section 2.5); or grammar-constrained decoding, which removes schema violations but adds 0.3 ms of mask work to every generated token.

  1. For the two rejection-and-retry designs, compute the latency a failed first attempt adds before its retry can start and the expected added latency per proposal. For grammar-constrained decoding, compute the added latency from mask work instead.
  2. Compute the daily decode time the service spends on failed attempts under post-hoc validation and under streaming validation with an honored cancel. How does the second figure change if the service ignores cancellation, and which of the runtime’s guarantees survive?
  3. Find the per-token mask overhead at which grammar-constrained decoding ties streaming validation on expected latency. Then argue which design to ship, naming one property none of the three establishes.

7. The same method, a different binding term (Intermediate; second lighthouse). The research agent (lighthouse 1.2) answers one question with 8 parallel workers. Each worker makes \(\TurnHorizon = 12\) calls, starts from \(\BasePromptLength = 6{,}000\) tokens, and on every turn appends a 5,000-token retrieved page plus its own 100-token output, so \(\TurnAppendLength = 5{,}100\). Use the floors and prices of problems 2 and 4, and ignore prefix reuse throughout.

  1. Compute the prompt tokens supplied per worker and across the question, the output tokens across the question, and input’s share of the uncached dollars.
  2. For a worker’s final call, compute the prefill and decode times at the floors. Which term of equation 6 binds, and how does that compare with the repair agent’s first turn in this chapter?
  3. One proposal copies the repair agent’s lever and halves every output to 50 tokens. Another keeps each retrieved page in full only for the call that reads it and replaces it afterward with a 400-token note. Compute the dollars each saves per question and choose one. What constraint of this lighthouse does neither calculation address?

8. Choosing a representation when the deadline moves (Advanced). A repair call starts from 9,000 context tokens, with the floors of problem 2 and the prices of problem 4. A whole-file response adds 300 instruction tokens and generates 1,200 output tokens; 98 percent apply to the expected version, and 60 percent of those pass the repair checks. A localized edit adds 100 instruction tokens and generates 120; 85 percent identify an applicable target, and 55 percent of those pass. Every attempt, whatever its outcome, also incurs a $0.05 check charge.

  1. Under an invocation deadline of 10 s, which representations can produce an eligible candidate?
  2. The deadline relaxes to 20 s. Compute \(\mathbb{E}[\AttemptCost]\), \(\AcceptanceProbability\), and \(\CostPerAcceptedTask\) (equation 1.2) for each design, and the estimated model time per accepted repair. Which design wins on each measure?
  3. Find the applicability rate at which the localized edit ties the whole-file design on \(\CostPerAcceptedTask\). Then critique the benchmark: which of the two measures should decide for an agent whose trajectory has a wall-clock budget, what could raise the localized design’s applicability without lengthening its output, and what single measurement would you add before choosing?

9. A truncated command at the irreversible end (Advanced; second lighthouse). The remediation agent (lighthouse 1.3) proposes 5,000 shell commands a month under an output allowance of 512 tokens, generated at \(\PerTokenDecodeTime = 10\) ms per token. Of these calls, 2 percent end TRUNCATED. Of the truncated fragments, 40 percent still parse as a complete command, as when --replicas=12 stops after --replicas=1. Executing a parseable fragment causes a wrong production change with probability 0.5, and each wrong change costs $20,000 to detect and reverse.

  1. Compute the expected wrong changes and loss per month for a runtime that dispatches anything that parses, and for one that applies the quarantining rule of section 2.5.
  2. For a truncated call, a blind retry at the same allowance truncates again with probability 0.9; a retry at a doubled allowance truncates with probability 0.1. Treating retries as independent and charging each attempt its full allowance as an upper bound, compute the expected attempts and decode time until a completed candidate under each policy.
  3. A colleague proposes a “repair parser” that closes truncated fragments so they can proceed without a retry. Critique the proposal using this lighthouse’s binding constraint, and redesign the runtime’s handling of TRUNCATED for this agent, stating what evidence a completed command still needs before it may execute.

What’s Next: From one candidate to deliberate computation
The contract ends at one prompt and one candidate, and a completed candidate can still rest on the wrong diagnosis. When the repair agent’s first patch extends a timeout that may not have caused the stall, the runtime can spend more before committing: a longer derivation, more sampled candidates, or a targeted test that observes the running pool. Each option buys a different kind of evidence, and only the test adds a fact the model did not already have. Test-Time Compute examines how to divide a limited budget among these options, how to select from a candidate pool without being fooled by a plausible failure, and when to stop.
